[← Hall of Foundations](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/index.html>) Exhibit 150 · y² = x³ + ax + b

A Gentle First Look

### Adding points
on a *curve*

Before elliptic curves become the engine of modern cryptography, they are just a graceful loop with one surprising trick: you can add two points on the curve and land on a third. This exhibit introduces that trick slowly, by hand, over the ordinary real numbers.

**Start here.** If the finite-field version felt like a jump, this is the on-ramp. Same rule, but drawn smoothly so you can see the geometry before it hides inside a field.

§1

#### Not an ellipse

A nonsingular Weierstrass cubic, together with its point at infinity, defines the group shown here. Its real points form a smooth symmetric plot; the number of connected components depends on the coefficients. The name comes from elliptic integrals, not from being an ellipse.

y² = x³ + ax + b

§2

#### The chord-and-tangent rule

To add points *P* and *Q*: draw the line through them, find the third point where it meets the curve, and reflect that across the x-axis. The reflection is *P + Q*. Tap two points and watch the construction draw itself.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="curve" viewBox="0 0 700 460" id="stage" role="img" aria-label="Interactive elliptic curve over the reals. Tap points to add them.">
          <g id="grid"></g>
          <path id="curvePath" fill="none" stroke="#F3EEDF" stroke-width="2.4"></path>
          <g id="construction"></g>
          <g id="points"></g>
        </svg>
```

Pick two points Reset

y² = x³ + x + 1 · **tap the curve**

tap once for *P*, again for *Q*. The chord, the third intersection, and the reflection to *P+Q* all draw in vermilion. Coordinates are computed from the real group law.

Keyboard alternative: choose two points on the curve to add. Point P   Point Q   Add P + Q

§3

#### Why an addition at all?

This rule isn't arbitrary — it turns the points of the curve into a [group](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/group-theory.html>), with all the structure that word carries: an identity (the "point at infinity"), inverses (the reflection), and associativity. That group is exactly what cryptography moves into a finite field to build keys on.

The group law supplies arithmetic. Security requires suitable finite-field parameters and a complete protocol.

The construction drawn above also has *exceptional cases*: two points sharing an x-coordinate have no chord with a finite slope, and a point added to itself needs the tangent instead. Any implementation must branch on those cases, or use **complete addition formulas** that are correct for every pair without branching — a guarantee stated for a particular curve class, not for curves in general. Whether a correct formula was implemented correctly, and whether the compiled result runs in constant time, are two further questions that a correct formula does not settle. [Four different promises about the same addition](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/finite-fields.html#assurance>) sets them side by side.

Where this leads

##### Two doors from here

Follow this into [Finite Fields](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/finite-fields.html>) to watch the corresponding arithmetic over a finite field. A scatter plot alone does not demonstrate discrete-log hardness. Or follow it up into [The Ground Beneath the Curve](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/modularity-exhibit.html>) to meet the deep number theory of **why these curves are so astonishingly well-behaved**.

Applied in: [ECDSA](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/ecdsa.html>) — this same chord-and-tangent group law, moved to a finite field, signing TLS certificates and blockchain transactions. The expression dG means scalar multiplication: repeated group addition, computed efficiently using methods such as doubling and addition.

#### Embedded script 1 (JavaScript)

Source text, not a record of every rendered state. Any ebook URL is replaced by a stable omission ID; redacted code is for inspection, not execution.

```javascript

(function () {
  "use strict";
  var svg = document.getElementById("stage");
  var NS = "http://www.w3.org/2000/svg";
  var W = 700, H = 460;
  var A = 1, B = 1;                 // single connected component
  var xmin = -1.4, xmax = 3.2, ymin = -4.2, ymax = 4.2;
  function sx(x){ return (x - xmin)/(xmax - xmin) * W; }
  function sy(y){ return H - (y - ymin)/(ymax - ymin) * H; }
  function make(tag, at){ var e=document.createElementNS(NS,tag); for(var k in at)e.setAttribute(k,at[k]); return e; }

  var grid = document.getElementById("grid");
  function axis(x1,y1,x2,y2,w,op){ grid.appendChild(make("line",{x1:x1,y1:y1,x2:x2,y2:y2,stroke:"#2e2e3a","stroke-width":w,"stroke-opacity":op})); }
  for (var gx=Math.ceil(xmin); gx<=xmax; gx++) axis(sx(gx),0,sx(gx),H,1, gx===0?0.9:0.35);
  for (var gy=Math.ceil(ymin); gy<=ymax; gy++) axis(0,sy(gy),W,sy(gy),1, gy===0?0.9:0.35);

  function fx(x){ return window.ECMath.curveY2(x, A, B); }
  var top=[], bot=[], step=0.012;
  for (var x=xmin; x<=xmax; x+=step){ var v=fx(x); if(v>=0){ var r=Math.sqrt(v); top.push([x,r]); bot.push([x,-r]); } }
  var d="";
  top.forEach(function(p,i){ d += (i?"L":"M")+sx(p[0]).toFixed(1)+" "+sy(p[1]).toFixed(1)+" "; });
  for (var j=bot.length-1; j>=0; j--){ d += "L"+sx(bot[j][0]).toFixed(1)+" "+sy(bot[j][1]).toFixed(1)+" "; }
  document.getElementById("curvePath").setAttribute("d", d.trim());

  var cons=document.getElementById("construction"), pts=document.getElementById("points"), readout=document.getElementById("readout");
  var picks=[];
  function onCurveNear(px,py){
    var best=null,bestd=1e9;
    for (var xx=xmin; xx<=xmax; xx+=0.004){ var v=fx(xx); if(v<0)continue; var r=Math.sqrt(v);
      [[xx,r],[xx,-r]].forEach(function(c){ var dd=(sx(c[0])-px)*(sx(c[0])-px)+(sy(c[1])-py)*(sy(c[1])-py); if(dd<bestd){bestd=dd;best=c;} }); }
    return best;
  }
  function dot(x,y,color,r){ return make("circle",{cx:sx(x),cy:sy(y),r:r||6,fill:color,stroke:"#1c1c22","stroke-width":2}); }
  function label(x,y,txt,color){ var t=make("text",{x:sx(x)+11,y:sy(y)-9,fill:color,"font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"15","font-weight":"700"}); t.textContent=txt; return t; }
  // Group law lives in ec-math.js (window.ECMath), proven in ec-math.test.js.
  function addPoints(P,Q){ return window.ECMath.ecAddReal(P, Q, A); }
  function drawConstruction(){
    cons.innerHTML=""; if(picks.length<2)return;
    var P=picks[0],Q=picks[1],res=addPoints(P,Q),third=res.third,sum=res.sum,m=res.slope;
    if(sum===null){
      cons.appendChild(make("line",{x1:sx(P[0]),y1:0,x2:sx(P[0]),y2:H,stroke:"#C85A5A","stroke-width":2}));
      readout.textContent="P + Q = ∞ — the point at infinity (the group identity).";
      return;
    }
    var yL=P[1]+m*(xmin-P[0]), yR=P[1]+m*(xmax-P[0]);
    cons.appendChild(make("line",{x1:sx(xmin),y1:sy(yL),x2:sx(xmax),y2:sy(yR),stroke:"#C85A5A","stroke-width":2,"stroke-opacity":0.85}));
    cons.appendChild(dot(third[0],third[1],"#d98a7a",5));
    cons.appendChild(make("line",{x1:sx(third[0]),y1:sy(third[1]),x2:sx(sum[0]),y2:sy(sum[1]),stroke:"#C85A5A","stroke-width":1.6,"stroke-dasharray":"4 4","stroke-opacity":0.8}));
    cons.appendChild(dot(sum[0],sum[1],"#C85A5A",7));
    cons.appendChild(label(sum[0],sum[1],"P+Q","#C85A5A"));
    readout.innerHTML="P+Q = ( <b>"+sum[0].toFixed(2)+"</b>, <b>"+sum[1].toFixed(2)+"</b> )";
  }
  function redrawPoints(){ pts.innerHTML=""; picks.forEach(function(p,i){ pts.appendChild(dot(p[0],p[1],"#F3EEDF",6)); pts.appendChild(label(p[0],p[1],i===0?"P":"Q","#F3EEDF")); }); }
  function handle(evt){
    var rect=svg.getBoundingClientRect();
    var cx=((evt.touches?evt.touches[0].clientX:evt.clientX)-rect.left)*(W/rect.width);
    var cy=((evt.touches?evt.touches[0].clientY:evt.clientY)-rect.top)*(H/rect.height);
    var p=onCurveNear(cx,cy); if(!p)return;
    if(picks.length>=2){ picks=[]; cons.innerHTML=""; }
    picks.push(p); redrawPoints();
    if(picks.length===2) drawConstruction();
    else readout.innerHTML="P = ( <b>"+p[0].toFixed(2)+"</b>, <b>"+p[1].toFixed(2)+"</b> ) · tap for Q";
  }
  svg.addEventListener("click",handle);

  // Touch: only intercept a deliberate tap, never a scroll. Track the start
  // point; movement past a small slop means the finger is scrolling and the
  // page's default behaviour is left alone.
  var touchStart=null, TAP_SLOP=12;
  svg.addEventListener("touchstart",function(e){
    if(e.touches.length!==1){ touchStart=null; return; }
    var t=e.touches[0];
    touchStart={x:t.clientX,y:t.clientY,scrolled:false};
  },{passive:true});
  svg.addEventListener("touchmove",function(e){
    if(!touchStart)return;
    var t=e.touches[0];
    if(Math.hypot(t.clientX-touchStart.x,t.clientY-touchStart.y)>TAP_SLOP) touchStart.scrolled=true;
  },{passive:true});
  svg.addEventListener("touchend",function(e){
    if(!touchStart||touchStart.scrolled){ touchStart=null; return; }
    e.preventDefault(); // confirmed tap: suppress the emulated click and act
    var t=e.changedTouches[0];
    handle({clientX:t.clientX,clientY:t.clientY});
    touchStart=null;
  },{passive:false});
  document.getElementById("reset").addEventListener("click",function(){ picks=[]; cons.innerHTML=""; pts.innerHTML=""; readout.innerHTML="y² = x³ + x + 1 · <b>tap the curve</b>"; });
  document.getElementById("pick").addEventListener("click",function(){ picks=[]; var Px=-0.6,Qx=1.2; picks.push([Px,Math.sqrt(fx(Px))]); picks.push([Qx,-Math.sqrt(fx(Qx))]); redrawPoints(); drawConstruction(); });

  // Keyboard / screen-reader fallback: populate the two <select>s with real
  // on-curve points; adding announces the sum via the aria-live readout.
  var selP=document.getElementById("selP"), selQ=document.getElementById("selQ");
  var sampleXs=[-0.6,-0.2,0.4,1.0,1.6,2.4], kbPoints=[];
  sampleXs.forEach(function(x){
    var v=fx(x); if(v<0)return;
    var r=Math.sqrt(v);
    kbPoints.push([x,r]); kbPoints.push([x,-r]);
  });
  kbPoints.forEach(function(p,i){
    var text="( "+p[0].toFixed(2)+", "+p[1].toFixed(2)+" )";
    var oP=document.createElement("option"); oP.value=i; oP.textContent=text; selP.appendChild(oP);
    var oQ=document.createElement("option"); oQ.value=i; oQ.textContent=text; selQ.appendChild(oQ);
  });
  selP.value="0";
  selQ.value=String(Math.min(3,kbPoints.length-1));
  document.getElementById("addKb").addEventListener("click",function(){
    picks=[kbPoints[+selP.value],kbPoints[+selQ.value]];
    redrawPoints();
    drawConstruction(); // updates the aria-live readout with P+Q
  });
})();
```

## P169 — Information & Entropy · Hall of Foundations

- Site path: `hall-of-foundations/entropy.html`
- Public page: https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/entropy.html
- Snapshot source SHA-256: `5766e7d2c51b2d7aa1865bf55e09a5408762c851dffe767adf6b3cd4d72757aa`

### Complete static body text and inline diagrams

Hidden/reveal panels and option labels are included. Script-generated text is supplied below and in the JavaScript/data appendices. Table cells are separated by vertical bars; superscripts/subscripts retain their HTML meaning. This is a text representation, not a screenshot.

<svg class="nav-logo-icon" viewBox="0 0 32 32" fill="none" aria-hidden="true">
        <circle cx="16" cy="16" r="14" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1.5"></circle>
        <circle cx="16" cy="16" r="8" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1"></circle>
        <circle cx="16" cy="16" r="2" fill="currentColor"></circle>
        <line x1="16" y1="2" x2="16" y2="6" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1.5"></line>
        <line x1="16" y1="26" x2="16" y2="30" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1.5"></line>

 The Cipher Museum](<https://ciphermuseum.com/index.html>)

- [Explore](<https://ciphermuseum.com/museum-map.html>)

- [Timeline](<https://ciphermuseum.com/timeline.html>)

[← Hall of Foundations](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/index.html>) Exhibit 151 · H = −Σ p log p

The Measure of Surprise

### Counting what
you *don't* know

Shannon gave uncertainty a number. Entropy measures how surprising a source is — how many bits of genuine unknowing it holds. It's why the one-time pad can achieve perfect secrecy, and why predictable passwords are vulnerable to guessing. Guessing cost also depends on the password distribution, derivation function and attack setting.

**Different measures answer different questions.** Shannon entropy measures average uncertainty. Min-entropy, guessing distributions and the cost of each trial are also relevant to attacks. None is a stand-alone security rating for a complete system.

§1

#### Bits of the unknown

A fair coin holds exactly one bit of entropy: two outcomes, equally likely. Bias the coin and the entropy drops — a coin that's 90% heads barely surprises you. Drag the bias and watch the entropy fall from its peak.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="curve" viewBox="0 0 700 380" id="hcurve" role="img" aria-label="Entropy of a biased coin as bias varies."></svg>
```

P(heads)  [Input pr: type=range; initial value=50]

—

Entropy peaks at **1 bit** when the coin is fair and collapses toward **0** as it becomes predictable. Maximum uncertainty means maximum secrecy-carrying capacity.

§2

#### Key length under a uniform model

These bars assume each character is selected independently and uniformly from the stated alphabet. Under that model, entropy is length × log₂(alphabet size). Human-chosen passwords do not generally follow this model: length and character variety alone do not determine resistance to guessing.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="curve" viewBox="0 0 700 340" id="keybar" role="img" aria-label="Entropy in bits for different password lengths and alphabets."></svg>
```

length  [Input lr: type=range; initial value=12]

—

Bars show total entropy in bits for three alphabets. As a rule of thumb, **128 bits** is the modern floor for a symmetric key — the dotted line.

§3

#### A flat histogram can hide a pattern

Write A–Z in order 100 times. Each letter occurs 100 times, yet the next letter is completely predictable. The [workbench](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/workbench.html>) reports an index of coincidence (IC) rounded to **0.0381**. The exact value is 26 × 100 × 99 / (2600 × 2599). Rearranging those same letters preserves the histogram and IC. Neither statistic measures their order.

To discuss entropy, specify a source distribution. In the cyclic source below, choose one of the 26 starting letters uniformly *once*, then continue around the alphabet forever. Compare that with choosing a fresh independent uniform letter at every position. Here n is a positive block length.

Uniform single-letter distributions, different sequence uncertainty

| Declared source | One position | Whole n-letter block | Entropy rate |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| One random cyclic phase | log₂ 26 ≈ 4.7004 bits | log₂ 26 bits: only 26 possible blocks | 0 bits per letter in the limit |
| Independent uniform letters | log₂ 26 ≈ 4.7004 bits | n log₂ 26 bits: 26ⁿ possible blocks | log₂ 26 bits per letter |

Entropy rate is the limiting block entropy divided by block length. For the cyclic source, 4.7004/n tends to zero: learning the first letter determines the rest. These are properties of the declared sources, not entropy estimates for a particular observed string.

Robert M. Gray, *Entropy and Information Theory*, corrected first edition, 26 June 2023 (original 1990), §2.2, printed pp.18–20, and §2.6, printed p.42. The alphabet/IC comparison is our worked application of the distinction between marginal and sequence entropy. Continue with Crypto Lab’s [Good Randomness](<https://systemslibrarian.github.io/crypto-lab-good-randomness/>) for deeper randomness experiments.

For a conditional-distribution control, try the [exact OTP secrecy experiment](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/one-time-pad.html#secrecy-model>): uniform plaintext can give a flat ciphertext histogram even with a biased pad. Compare the chosen prior, posterior and pairwise ciphertext distance; the same page enumerates why two individually hidden messages can leak jointly when a pad is reused.

§4

#### Average uncertainty versus the first guess

Shannon entropy averages surprise across outcomes. Min-entropy asks about the most likely outcome: **H<sub>min</sub> = −log₂(P<sub>best</sub>)**, where P<sub>best</sub> is the success probability of the best single guess. Neither specifies the cost of testing a guess.

Consider an n-bit source that outputs the all-zero string with probability a, and otherwise draws a uniform n-bit string. The uniform branch can also produce zeros, so P<sub>best</sub> = a + (1−a)/2ⁿ. Each other string has probability q = (1−a)/2ⁿ. Its exact Shannon entropy is −P<sub>best</sub> log₂ P<sub>best</sub> − (2ⁿ−1)q log₂ q, with 0 log₂ 0 treated as zero.

Two declared 256-bit sources

| Source | Shannon entropy | Best first guess | Min-entropy |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Uniform (a = 0) | 256 bits | Success 2⁻²⁵⁶ | 256 bits |
| Mixture (a = 1/2) | Approximately 129 bits | Guess all zeros: success 1/2 + 2⁻²⁵⁷ | Approximately 1 bit |

The mixture has substantial average uncertainty, yet one guess succeeds about half the time. This is a constructed distribution, not a measured password population; 2 raised to the Shannon entropy is not a “guesses required” formula. Deterministic hashing cannot manufacture missing source entropy.

Adapted from Salil P. Vadhan, *Pseudorandomness*, *Foundations and Trends in Theoretical Computer Science* 7(1–3), volume dated 2011, copyright 2012, §6.1.2, printed pp.171–172 (Definition 6.7 and Lemma 6.8). Vadhan uses a .99/.01 mixture; the half-and-half values here are our teaching preset. Crypto Lab’s [Noise to Numbers](<https://systemslibrarian.github.io/crypto-lab-noise-to-numbers/>) offers a deeper exploration of randomness sources.

§5

#### A shorter secret after partial leakage

Alice and Bob share two independent fair bits X₁ and X₂. An observer learns one bit and which position it came from; choose that position independently and fairly. Alice and Bob do not know which position leaked. Their shared output K = X₁ XOR X₂ is still a fair secret bit under this specific leakage model.

All eight equally likely outcomes (probability 1/8 each)

| X₁X₂ | Revealed position | Observed bit | K = parity |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 00 | 1 | 0 | 0 |
| 00 | 2 | 0 | 0 |
| 01 | 1 | 0 | 1 |
| 01 | 2 | 1 | 1 |
| 10 | 1 | 1 | 1 |
| 10 | 2 | 0 | 1 |
| 11 | 1 | 1 | 0 |
| 11 | 2 | 1 | 0 |

Group rows by what the observer knows: (position, observed bit). Every group contains one K = 0 and one K = 1. Thus K is uniform and independent of that observation. For example, seeing position 1 equal to 0 leaves 00 and 01 equally likely, giving opposite parities.

**Negative control: leak parity instead.** If the observer learns X₁ XOR X₂, the proposed output K is completely known: their first guess succeeds with probability 1. XOR does not remove arbitrary leakage.

Privacy amplification aims to obtain a shorter, almost-uniform secret independent of the observer’s side information and the public extractor choice. The guarantees depend on the joint source/leakage model and remaining uncertainty. This fixed table enumerates a model; it generates no operational secret keys.

Himanshu Tyagi and Shun Watanabe, *Information-theoretic Cryptography*, Cambridge University Press, 2023, §7.3, printed p.124, Example 7.9 and Definition 7.10; pp.125–127 develop conditional min-entropy and Theorem 7.13. The leaked-parity control is our contrasting example. See also the [XOR glossary entry](<https://ciphermuseum.com/glossary.html#gl-X>).

§6

#### Pooling many keys measures the collection, not the message

Research datasets of homophonic ciphers are built by encrypting many plaintexts, each under a key generated independently for it. Measure the entropy of all that ciphertext together and you get a large number. It is easy to read that number as a statement about how well any one message is hidden. It is not.

Here is a fully enumerable case. Three plaintext letters A, B, C; two homophones each; sixty letters per message, with the three letters equally frequent and the two homophones alternating evenly. Four independent keys draw their code symbols from **disjoint** inventories, so no symbol is shared.

The same four messages, measured two ways

| What is measured | Distinct code symbols | Entropy per code symbol |
| --- | --- | --- |
| One message under one fixed key | 6 | log₂ 6 = 2.5850 bits |
| All four messages pooled | 24 | log₂ 24 = 4.5850 bits |

The pooled figure is exactly **2 bits higher**, and 2 = log₂ 4 is the number of keys. Nothing about any single message changed. An attacker holding one of these messages still faces a three-letter cipher with six code symbols; the extra two bits only record which of the four keys a symbol came from, which is not a secret the message is keeping.

**The bound for a realistic size.** Take the usual research setting: 26 plaintext letters, at most five homophones for each. One fixed key then has **at most 26 × 5 = 130 code types**, so the entropy of its code-symbol distribution is at most **log₂ 130 ≈ 7.022 bits** per symbol — and only if all 130 were equally likely, which proportional homophone allocation never quite achieves. A pooled alphabet of eleven thousand codes gives log₂ 11000 ≈ 13.425 bits, almost double. That larger number is a property of the collection, not of the cipher a reader of one letter is facing.

**Status of this example:** a mathematical consequence of the stated model, worked and checked here. It is not a replication of any published dataset measurement, and it does not say a published pooled figure is wrong — a pooled statistic is simply a different statistic, and it cannot establish per-message uniformity or security.

§7

#### Unicity distance answers a narrower question than it looks

Shannon’s unicity distance estimates how much ciphertext is needed before only one key is expected to yield a sensible plaintext. It is an expectation computed from two assumptions: the size of the key space, and the redundancy of the plaintext language. Change either assumption and the estimate changes.

Three things it does not do.

- **It does not certify a proposed reading.** The estimate says spurious readings become unlikely past some length. It says nothing about whether the particular reading in front of you is the right one. That still needs evidence outside the cipher: corroborating documents, the archive, the hand, the history.

- **It does not promise an efficient solver.** A unique solution can exist and remain out of reach. Unicity is an information-theoretic count of how many keys survive, not a statement about the work of finding the survivor.

- **It does not cover alternative systems.** The calculation fixes a cipher family and a key space. It cannot rule out that some different enciphering scheme also produces readable text.

The published analysis of the Zodiac 340 cipher makes the dependence visible: its author computes the plaintext-language entropy under three different assumptions — standard English, the Z340 plaintext alone, and Zodiac’s whole correspondence — and reports the resulting unicity distance as a *range*, at most 152 characters, rather than a single figure.

**A rule that follows from both sections.** Enlarging the pooled code alphabet changes a dataset statistic. It does not make any individual message easier or harder to recover, and a cryptanalytic result that improves when the pool grows is measuring the pool, not the cipher. The museum’s [research-evaluation v2](<https://ciphermuseum.com/data/research/v2/README.md>) keeps those tasks apart for exactly this reason.

Micaella Bruton, Meriem Beloucif and Beáta Megyesi, “Bridging the Low Resource Gap in Historical Cryptology: A Multilingual Diachronic Synthetic Dataset for Reproducible Cryptanalysis,” [RESOURCEFUL 2026, pp. 13–24](<https://aclanthology.org/2026.resourceful-4.2/>): independently generated per-text homophonic keys. Joachim von zur Gathen, [“Unicity distance of the Zodiac-340 cipher,” ePrint 2021/1620](<https://eprint.iacr.org/2021/1620>) (Cryptologia 47(5), 2023): the three language-entropy assumptions and the resulting range. Continue with [the homophonic exhibit](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/homophonic.html>) and [the Evidence Workbench](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/evidence-workbench.html>).

§8

#### The one-time pad's perfect secret

A pad chosen uniformly and independently of the message, kept secret, as long as the message and used once provides perfect secrecy for fixed-length messages. Entropy is part of that argument, but a high-entropy string that is public or related to the message is not a secure pad.

Perfect secrecy exists. It just costs one bit of true randomness for every bit you want to hide.

Where this touches your keys

##### Uncertainty and attack cost

The [One-Time Pad](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/one-time-pad.html>)’s perfect secrecy is this measure made physical. A cipher fed a predictable key can be vulnerable to searches over likely candidates, with cost depending on how candidates can be tested. Entropy is why **the random number generator matters as much as the cipher**.

Security bits estimate attack cost under a specified model; they are not the same quantity as Shannon entropy. AES-128 has a 128-bit key space and is intended to provide roughly 128-bit classical security. A suitable 256-bit elliptic-curve group has generic discrete-log attacks costing roughly 2¹²⁸ group operations. A source's Shannon entropy alone does not specify its password-guessing cost or a scheme's computational security.

Where the ceiling is reached: [the One-Time Pad](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/one-time-pad.html>) uses one independently uniform secret key bit per message bit in the binary construction. [Vernam’s 1917 combining machine](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/vernam.html>) supplies a mechanical precursor; its security depends on the tape, and a repeating tape is not a one-time pad.

```javascript

(function(){
  "use strict";
  var NS="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg";
  function el(t,a){var e=document.createElementNS(NS,t);for(var k in a)e.setAttribute(k,a[k]);return e;}

  // ---- §1 entropy curve ----
  var hcurve=document.getElementById("hcurve"), pr=document.getElementById("pr"), hread=document.getElementById("hread");
  function H2(p){ if(p<=0||p>=1)return 0; return -(p*Math.log2(p)+(1-p)*Math.log2(1-p)); }
  function drawH(){
    var W=700,H=380,pad=48; hcurve.innerHTML="";
    function px(p){return pad+p*(W-2*pad);}
    function py(v){return H-pad-(v/1.05)*(H-2*pad);}
    hcurve.appendChild(el("line",{x1:pad,y1:H-pad,x2:W-pad,y2:H-pad,stroke:"#F3EEDF","stroke-width":1.5}));
    hcurve.appendChild(el("line",{x1:pad,y1:pad,x2:pad,y2:H-pad,stroke:"#F3EEDF","stroke-width":1.5}));
    var d="";
    for(var p=0.001;p<1;p+=0.004){ d+=(d?"L":"M")+px(p).toFixed(1)+" "+py(H2(p)).toFixed(1)+" "; }
    hcurve.appendChild(el("path",{d:d.trim(),fill:"none",stroke:"#5AC8A0","stroke-width":2.6}));
    var cp=+pr.value/100, cy=H2(cp);
    hcurve.appendChild(el("line",{x1:px(cp),y1:H-pad,x2:px(cp),y2:py(cy),stroke:"#C85A5A","stroke-width":1.5,"stroke-dasharray":"4 4"}));
    hcurve.appendChild(el("circle",{cx:px(cp),cy:py(cy),r:7,fill:"#C85A5A",stroke:"#1c1c22","stroke-width":2}));
    var l1=el("text",{x:px(0.5),y:py(1)-12,fill:"#5AC8A0","font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"12","text-anchor":"middle","font-weight":"700"});
    l1.textContent="1 bit (fair)"; hcurve.appendChild(l1);
    hread.innerHTML="P(heads) = <b>"+cp.toFixed(2)+"</b> · entropy = <b>"+cy.toFixed(3)+"</b> bits";
  }
  pr.addEventListener("input",drawH); drawH();

  // ---- §2 key bars ----
  var keybar=document.getElementById("keybar"), lr=document.getElementById("lr"), kread=document.getElementById("kread");
  var sets=[{name:"digits (10)",n:10,c:"#D4B765"},{name:"lower (26)",n:26,c:"#5AC8A0"},{name:"printable non-space ASCII (94)",n:94,c:"#C85A5A"}];
  function drawKeys(){
    var W=700,H=340,pad=54; keybar.innerHTML="";
    var L=+lr.value;
    var vals=sets.map(function(s){return L*Math.log2(s.n);});
    var maxV=Math.max(256, Math.max.apply(null,vals)*1.1);
    function by(v){return (v/maxV)*(H-2*pad);}
    keybar.appendChild(el("line",{x1:pad,y1:H-pad,x2:W-pad,y2:H-pad,stroke:"#F3EEDF","stroke-width":1.5}));
    // 128-bit floor line
    var floorY=H-pad-by(128);
    keybar.appendChild(el("line",{x1:pad,y1:floorY,x2:W-pad,y2:floorY,stroke:"#F3EEDF","stroke-width":1.2,"stroke-dasharray":"5 5","stroke-opacity":0.6}));
    var ft=el("text",{x:W-pad,y:floorY-6,fill:"#D5CDDF","font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"11","text-anchor":"end"});
    ft.textContent="128-bit floor"; keybar.appendChild(ft);
    var bw=110, gap=90, x0=140;
    sets.forEach(function(s,i){
      var v=vals[i], h=by(v), x=x0+i*(bw+gap);
      keybar.appendChild(el("rect",{x:x,y:H-pad-h,width:bw,height:h,fill:s.c,"fill-opacity":v>=128?1:0.55}));
      var vt=el("text",{x:x+bw/2,y:H-pad-h-8,fill:s.c,"font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"14","text-anchor":"middle","font-weight":"700"});
      vt.textContent=Math.round(v)+" b"; keybar.appendChild(vt);
      var nt=el("text",{x:x+bw/2,y:H-pad+20,fill:"#D5CDDF","font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"11","text-anchor":"middle"});
      nt.textContent=s.name; keybar.appendChild(nt);
    });
    kread.innerHTML="length <b>"+L+"</b> · full-ASCII ≈ <b>"+Math.round(vals[2])+"</b> bits "+(vals[2]>=128?"✓":"(below floor)");
  }
  lr.addEventListener("input",drawKeys); drawKeys();
})();
```

[← Hall of Foundations](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/index.html>) Exhibit 145 · aᵠ⁽ⁿ⁾ ≡ 1

The Engine of RSA Decryption

### Why RSA
*undoes itself*

RSA encrypts by raising a message to a power, and decrypts by raising the result to another. That these two operations cancel — that you land back on the exact message you started with — is not luck. It's a 400-year-old theorem of Fermat, generalized by Euler.

**Small but essential.** This is the shortest exhibit in the hall, because the idea is compact: one congruence explains why RSA works at all. Watch a real message make the round trip below.

§1

#### Fermat's little theorem

For any prime *p* and any *a* not divisible by *p*, raising *a* to the (p−1) power lands exactly on 1, modulo *p*. Every base, no exceptions.

aᵖ⁻¹ ≡ 1 (mod p)

Euler generalized it from primes to any modulus *n*, replacing p−1 with φ(n) — the count of numbers below *n* that share no factor with it. This is the version RSA runs on.

a<sup>φ(n)</sup> ≡ 1 (mod n) when gcd(a, n) = 1

§2

#### The round trip

RSA picks exponents *e* and *d* that are inverses modulo φ(n), so that e·d ≡ 1. Then encrypting (raise to *e*) and decrypting (raise to *d*) compose to raising by e·d — which, by Euler's theorem, does nothing at all. The message returns untouched. Type a small number and send it through.

One honest caveat: Euler's theorem as stated needs gcd(*m*, *n*) = 1, and with this toy key a few messages — the multiples of 61 or 53 — fail that test. RSA still round-trips them, but the reason is slightly different: you check the congruence separately modulo *p* and modulo *q*, where it holds either trivially or by Fermat, then glue the two halves back together with the Chinese Remainder Theorem. Real implementations also use Carmichael's λ(*n*) rather than φ(*n*) to pick *d*; it divides φ(*n*) and yields the same round trip with a potentially smaller exponent.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="curve" viewBox="0 0 700 300" id="trip" role="img" aria-label="A message encrypted then decrypted, returning to itself."></svg>
```

message m  [Input mr: type=range; initial value=65]   Encrypt → Decrypt

—

Toy key: p = 61, q = 53, n = 3233, e = 17, d = 2753. Real RSA uses the same arithmetic with much larger moduli, for example a 2048-bit *n*; the required size depends on the security target and applicable policy. The ciphertext is genuine modular exponentiation, computed live.

Encryption and decryption aren't opposites by design — they're opposites by theorem.

Where this touches your keys

##### The reason the mailbox opens

Factoring the modulus breaks RSA; the converse equivalence between general RSA inversion and factoring is not known. Correctness is a separate question, supplied here by modular arithmetic and the Chinese remainder theorem. This raw textbook-RSA round trip lacks secure encodings and must not be used for real encryption.

**Sources and scope:** [RFC 8017 (2016)](<https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8017.html>), RSA key relations and complete encryption/signature schemes.

Continue with [RSA](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/rsa.html>) or explore [modular arithmetic](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/modular-arithmetic.html>).

#### Embedded script 1 (JavaScript)

Source text, not a record of every rendered state. Any ebook URL is replaced by a stable omission ID; redacted code is for inspection, not execution.

```javascript

(function(){
  "use strict";
  var NS="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg";
  function el(t,a){var e=document.createElementNS(NS,t);for(var k in a)e.setAttribute(k,a[k]);return e;}
  // Modular exponentiation lives in crypto-algebra.js, proven in crypto-algebra.test.js.
  var powmod=window.CryptoAlgebra.powmod;
  var n=3233,e=17,d=2753;

  var trip=document.getElementById("trip"), mr=document.getElementById("mr"), tread=document.getElementById("tread");

  function stageNode(x,label,val,color){
    var g=el("g",{});
    g.appendChild(el("rect",{x:x-70,y:110,width:140,height:80,rx:4,
      fill:"#1c1c22",stroke:color,"stroke-width":2}));
    var l=el("text",{x:x,y:100,fill:"#D5CDDF","font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace",
      "font-size":"12","text-anchor":"middle"}); l.textContent=label;
    var v=el("text",{x:x,y:158,fill:color,"font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace",
      "font-size":"26","font-weight":"700","text-anchor":"middle"}); v.textContent=val;
    g.appendChild(l); g.appendChild(v); return g;
  }
  function arrow(x1,x2,label){
    var g=el("g",{});
    g.appendChild(el("line",{x1:x1,y1:150,x2:x2,y2:150,stroke:"#F3EEDF","stroke-width":2}));
    g.appendChild(el("polygon",{points:(x2)+",150 "+(x2-10)+",144 "+(x2-10)+",156",fill:"#F3EEDF"}));
    var t=el("text",{x:(x1+x2)/2,y:138,fill:"#C85A5A","font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace",
      "font-size":"13","font-weight":"700","text-anchor":"middle"}); t.textContent=label;
    g.appendChild(t); return g;
  }

  function draw(animate){
    var m=+mr.value;
    var c=powmod(m,e,n);
    var back=powmod(c,d,n);
    trip.innerHTML="";
    trip.appendChild(stageNode(120,"message m",m,"#F3EEDF"));
    trip.appendChild(arrow(200,340,"^"+e+" mod n"));
    trip.appendChild(stageNode(410,"ciphertext",c,"#C85A5A"));
    trip.appendChild(arrow(490,630,"^"+d+" mod n"));
    trip.appendChild(stageNode(580,"recovered",back,"#5AC8A0"));
    tread.innerHTML="m = <b>"+m+"</b> → c = <b>"+c+"</b> → <b style='color:#5AC8A0'>"+back+"</b> "+(back===m?"✓":"✗");
  }
  mr.addEventListener("input",function(){ draw(false); });
  document.getElementById("send").addEventListener("click",function(){ draw(true); });
  draw(false);
})();
```

[← Hall of Foundations](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/index.html>) Exhibit 142 · 𝔽ₚ

The Arithmetic World Everything Happens In

### When the curve
*shatters*

The elliptic curve you saw drawn as a smooth loop doesn't live on paper when it does real work. It lives over a finite field — a world with only finitely many numbers, where the curve becomes a scatter of dots. Suitable large finite-field groups can support hard discrete-log problems; the tiny groups here do not.

**Arithmetic, not a security test.** The real and finite-field pictures use related formulas, but apparent visual disorder does not prove discrete-log hardness. Curve choice, subgroup order, validation and implementation all matter.

§1

#### A field with no infinity

A finite field 𝔽ₚ is arithmetic modulo a prime *p*: the only numbers are 0, 1, …, p−1, and everything wraps around. Add, subtract, multiply, and — crucially — divide, all staying inside that finite set. Every nonzero element has an inverse, which is what makes it a *field* rather than just a ring.

𝔽ₚ = { 0, 1, 2, …, p−1 } with arithmetic mod p

The same elliptic-curve equation, y² = x³ + ax + b, now asks which pairs (x, y) satisfy it modulo the prime. The group also includes an identity called the point at infinity; that identity is not an extra field element.

§2

#### The curve, over 𝔽ₚ

Below is y² = x³ + 2x + 2 over 𝔽ₚ. Change *p* and watch the point set redraw. Notice the vertical symmetry — for every point (x, y) there's a mirror (x, p−y) — the finite-field ghost of the real curve's reflection.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="curve" viewBox="0 0 700 500" id="scatter" role="img" aria-label="Elliptic curve points over a finite field, as a scatter plot."></svg>
```

prime p
- 23

- 37

- 61

- 97 [selected]

- 127

—

Every dot is a genuine solution of y² ≡ x³ + 2x + 2 (mod p), computed exactly. The count is the **order** of the curve — the size of the group your key lives in.

§3

#### Adding points in the dark

The chord-and-tangent rule still works — but algebraically, not geometrically. Pick a starting point *G* and repeatedly add it to itself. Over the reals you could watch it slide along the curve. Here it jumps. Press *step* and follow G, 2G, 3G… as they scatter with no visible pattern.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="curve" viewBox="0 0 700 500" id="walk" role="img" aria-label="Repeated point addition over a finite field, jumping unpredictably."></svg>
```

Step · add G Run to 12G Reset

**k = 0**

You can see *k* and where *kG* landed. The discrete log problem is the reverse: given only the landing spot, recover *k*. This tiny group is easy to search; the plot alone tells us nothing about cryptographic hardness.

Forward scalar multiplication is efficient. Generic reverse attacks take roughly the square root of the subgroup order, with special curves permitting faster attacks. That distinction is one algebraic ingredient in a secure protocol.

§4

#### Four different promises about the same addition

The arithmetic above is correct and it is also a teaching model. Production elliptic-curve code carries claims this exhibit does not, and the claims are not interchangeable. Four of them are worth separating.

What each assurance covers, and what it leaves open

| Claim | What it means | What it does not cover |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Exceptional cases handled | The affine chord-and-tangent rule has no slope when the two points share an x-coordinate, so an implementation must branch: identity, inverse pair, doubling, general case. | Branching is where bugs and timing differences live. Handling a case is not handling it in constant time. |
| Complete addition formulas | One branch-free expression that is correct for *every* pair of points, including the identity and a point with itself. Renes, Costello and Batina give such formulas for short Weierstrass curves. | The completeness guarantee holds for a stated curve class — prime-order short Weierstrass curves over fields of characteristic other than 2 and 3. Outside that class the guarantee does not transfer. Costs depend on the formula, coordinates, field arithmetic and workload; completeness does not specify one universal multiplication cost. |
| Functional-correctness proof | A machine-checked argument that a particular implementation computes the specified mathematical function, including the bounds that keep intermediate values in range. Fiat-Cryptography produces field arithmetic this way. | A proof has a boundary. The compiler, the pretty-printer and anything below them are trusted, not proved, and the proof says nothing about timing. |
| Constant-time assurance | Changing secrets must not change the observable behavior in the stated execution and observation model. Public-dependent work and timing noise do not require identical wall-clock durations. | The boundary includes the compiler's emitted code, JIT or translation, instruction behavior and hardware. A compiler can introduce a secret-dependent branch; a data-memory-dependent prefetcher can create an observable access even without an explicit secret-indexed program load. Name the target and observations: branch-free code or a timing test alone does not prove universal non-leakage. |

**Where that leaves the code on this page.** The museum’s `ec-math.js` is affine, branching, and uses a linear search to invert a field element because the fields here are tiny teaching examples. It is **not production-ready, not constant-time, and not formally verified**, and it is not trying to be. What it *is* is checked: the museum’s test suite compares its result against an independent implementation of the complete projective formulas on all 361 ordered pairs of points on the prime-order curve y² = x³ + 2x + 2 over 𝔽₁₇, identity, inverses and doublings included. Agreement on a tiny curve is evidence about these formulas on this curve; it is not an assurance claim about anything else.

Property testing, proof and timing assurance answer different questions, and passing one says nothing about the others. For side-channel behaviour as a measured phenomenon rather than a source-code property, see [what a power-analysis result actually shows](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/modern-attack-evidence.html#power-leakage>) and Crypto Lab’s [Power Trace](<https://systemslibrarian.github.io/crypto-lab-power-trace/>), which computes those statistics over simulated leakage.

Joost Renes, Craig Costello and Lejla Batina, [“Complete Addition Formulas for Prime Order Elliptic Curves,” EUROCRYPT 2016](<https://eprint.iacr.org/2015/1060>): the curve class the completeness guarantee is stated for, and its cost. Andres Erbsen, Jade Philipoom, Jason Gross, Robert Sloan and Adam Chlipala, [“Simple High-Level Code for Cryptographic Arithmetic — With Proofs, Without Compromises,” IEEE S&P 2019](<https://doi.org/10.1109/SP.2019.00005>): the proof pipeline, its bounds reasoning, and the trusted compiler and pretty-printer at its edge.

A formula-cost example: [“Faster complete formulas and their application to curves with efficient endomorphisms”](<https://eprint.iacr.org/2026/2667>) (2026 preprint), §2/Table 1, PDF pp. 2–3, rewrites the final cross-product block with five instead of six field multiplications, trading one multiplication for five field additions. These are different operation types, not one fewer total operation. Completeness retains the original curve/subgroup conditions. The paper's fixed-base improvement and variable-base slowdown are different author-measured library workloads (§7/Table 8), not browser benchmarks. Our selectable toy curves are not all prime-order; the F17 oracle's guarantee is not transferred to every selection.

Thomas Pornin, [“Constant-Time Code: The Pessimist Case”](<https://eprint.iacr.org/2025/435>), note dated 6 March 2025, §§1–2,6: reported Clang 18.1.3 `-O3` compiler example and bounded useful practices, not a universal impossibility theorem. Sprengers et al., [“SecDMP”](<https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37454>), extended arXiv v1, 27 October 2026, §§V–VII and Appendix C: baseline-relative hardware non-leakage under a fixed secret partition. [Read the prefetcher evidence and its assumptions](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/modern-attack-evidence.html#dmp>).

Where this touches your keys

##### This is the ground floor of ECC

Real elliptic-curve cryptography uses carefully selected large fields, curves and prime-order subgroups. For common 256-bit prime-order groups, generic discrete-log attacks take roughly the square root of the group order, not a search through every field element. These tiny plots are readily enumerable and demonstrate arithmetic, not security.

[AES](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/aes.html>) uses a finite field too, but a different *kind* of one, and the distinction is worth drawing. This exhibit builds GF(*p*) — arithmetic modulo a prime. The AES S-box lives in GF(2⁸), an extension field of 256 elements whose members are polynomials, added bitwise and multiplied modulo a fixed irreducible polynomial. Same guarantee — every nonzero element has an inverse — reached by different construction, and the S-box maps zero to zero for the inversion step, takes the multiplicative inverse for every nonzero input, and then applies an affine map. The polynomial machinery is next door in [Polynomial Rings](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/polynomial-rings.html>).

Companion: [The Ground Beneath the Curve](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/modularity-exhibit.html>) shows the smooth real-number version and the deep theory of why these curves are so well-behaved.

**Sources and scope:** [NIST SP 800-186 (2023)](<https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/186/final>) specifies recommended elliptic-curve domain parameters; the small groups here are teaching examples.

#### Embedded script 1 (JavaScript)

Source text, not a record of every rendered state. Any ebook URL is replaced by a stable omission ID; redacted code is for inspection, not execution.

```javascript

(function(){
  "use strict";
  var NS="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg";
  var A=2, B=2;   // y^2 = x^3 + 2x + 2

  function el(tag,at){var e=document.createElementNS(NS,tag);for(var k in at)e.setAttribute(k,at[k]);return e;}
  // Curve math lives in ec-math.js (window.ECMath), proven in ec-math.test.js.
  var EC=window.ECMath;
  function mod(n,p){return EC.mod(n,p);}
  function points(p){return EC.ecPoints(A,B,p);}
  function addFp(P,Q,p){return EC.ecAdd(P,Q,A,p);}

  // ---------- scatter plot ----------
  var scatter=document.getElementById("scatter");
  var count=document.getElementById("count");
  function drawScatter(p){
    scatter.innerHTML="";
    var pad=32, W=700, H=500, span=Math.max(W,H)-2*pad;
    var cell=Math.min((W-2*pad)/p,(H-2*pad)/p);
    var ox=(W-cell*p)/2, oy=(H-cell*p)/2;
    // faint grid frame
    scatter.appendChild(el("rect",{x:ox,y:oy,width:cell*p,height:cell*p,
      fill:"none",stroke:"#2e2e3a","stroke-width":1}));
    // axis at mid isn't meaningful mod p; show border only
    var pts=points(p);
    pts.forEach(function(pt){
      var cx=ox+(pt[0]+0.5)*cell;
      var cy=oy+(p-1-pt[1]+0.5)*cell; // flip so y grows upward
      scatter.appendChild(el("circle",{cx:cx.toFixed(1),cy:cy.toFixed(1),
        r:Math.max(1.6,cell*0.32).toFixed(1),fill:"#F3EEDF","fill-opacity":0.85}));
    });
    count.innerHTML="p = <b>"+p+"</b> &nbsp;·&nbsp; <b>"+(pts.length+1)+"</b> points (with ∞)";
  }

  // ---------- walk ----------
  var walk=document.getElementById("walk");
  var kread=document.getElementById("kread");
  var Wp=97, G=null, cur=null, k=0, history=[], walkInterval=null;
  function stopWalk(){
    if(walkInterval!==null){ clearInterval(walkInterval); walkInterval=null; }
  }
  function findG(p){ var pts=points(p); return pts.length?pts[Math.floor(pts.length*0.18)]:null; }
  function drawWalk(){
    walk.innerHTML="";
    var p=Wp, W=700,H=500,pad=32;
    var cell=Math.min((W-2*pad)/p,(H-2*pad)/p);
    var ox=(W-cell*p)/2, oy=(H-cell*p)/2;
    walk.appendChild(el("rect",{x:ox,y:oy,width:cell*p,height:cell*p,fill:"none",stroke:"#2e2e3a","stroke-width":1}));
    var pts=points(p);
    pts.forEach(function(pt){
      var cx=ox+(pt[0]+0.5)*cell, cy=oy+(p-1-pt[1]+0.5)*cell;
      walk.appendChild(el("circle",{cx:cx.toFixed(1),cy:cy.toFixed(1),
        r:Math.max(1.4,cell*0.28).toFixed(1),fill:"#F3EEDF","fill-opacity":0.28}));
    });
    // draw history path
    for(var i=0;i<history.length;i++){
      var pt=history[i]; if(pt===null) continue;
      var cx=ox+(pt[0]+0.5)*cell, cy=oy+(p-1-pt[1]+0.5)*cell;
      var isLast=(i===history.length-1);
      if(i>0 && history[i-1]!==null){
        var pv=history[i-1];
        var px=ox+(pv[0]+0.5)*cell, py=oy+(p-1-pv[1]+0.5)*cell;
        walk.appendChild(el("line",{x1:px,y1:py,x2:cx,y2:cy,
          stroke:"#C85A5A","stroke-width":1.4,"stroke-opacity":0.5,"stroke-dasharray":"3 3"}));
      }
      walk.appendChild(el("circle",{cx:cx.toFixed(1),cy:cy.toFixed(1),
        r:isLast?7:4.5,fill:isLast?"#C85A5A":"#d98a7a",stroke:"#1c1c22","stroke-width":1.5}));
      if(isLast){
        var t=el("text",{x:cx+11,y:cy-9,fill:"#C85A5A",
          "font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"15","font-weight":"700"});
        t.textContent=k+"G"; walk.appendChild(t);
      }
    }
  }
  function stepWalk(){
    if(G===null) G=findG(Wp);
    if(k===0){ cur=G; k=1; history=[G]; }
    else { cur=addFp(cur,G,Wp); k++; history.push(cur); if(history.length>13)history.shift(); }
    kread.innerHTML="<b>k = "+k+"</b> &nbsp; kG = "+(cur?("("+cur[0]+", "+cur[1]+")"):"∞");
    drawWalk();
  }
  function resetWalk(){ stopWalk(); k=0; cur=null; history=[]; G=findG(Wp); kread.innerHTML="<b>k = 0</b>"; drawWalk(); }

  document.getElementById("step").addEventListener("click",function(){ stopWalk(); stepWalk(); });
  document.getElementById("run").addEventListener("click",function(){
    resetWalk();
    if(window.matchMedia&&window.matchMedia("(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)").matches){
      for(var n=0;n<12;n++) stepWalk();   // same end state, no animation
      return;
    }
    var n=0; walkInterval=setInterval(function(){
      stepWalk();
      if(++n>=12){ clearInterval(walkInterval); walkInterval=null; }
    },160);
  });
  document.getElementById("wreset").addEventListener("click",resetWalk);
  document.getElementById("psel").addEventListener("change",function(e){ drawScatter(+e.target.value); });

  drawScatter(97);
  resetWalk();
})();
```

[← Hall of Foundations](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/index.html>) Exhibit 149 · ⟨g⟩

The Grammar of Public-Key Cryptography

### The vocabulary
everything *shares*

Groups, generators, order. These three words describe the structure half of modern cryptography quietly depends on. A group is a set with one well-behaved operation; a generator is an element whose repeated application reaches everything; the order is how many steps that takes.

**The unifying abstraction.** Diffie–Hellman's clock, elliptic-curve points, RSA's invertible residues under multiplication — these look different but are all *groups*. The vocabulary helps explain these examples; it does not make their algorithms or security assumptions interchangeable.

§1

#### Generators and the ground they cover

Take the numbers 0 to n−1 with addition that wraps around — the group ℤ/nℤ. Pick a generator *g* and keep adding it to itself: g, 2g, 3g, … Some choices sweep through *every* element before returning to 0; others get trapped in a small sub-cycle. Watch which is which.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="curve" viewBox="0 0 700 520" id="grp" role="img" aria-label="A cyclic group with a generator sweeping around the ring."></svg>
```

group size n
- 10

- 12 [selected]

- 15

- 16
   generator g   Trace ⟨g⟩

—

When ⟨g⟩ covers all n elements, **g is a generator** and the group is cyclic under it. Otherwise g reaches only a **subgroup** — and its order divides n (Lagrange's theorem, live).

§2

#### Why cryptographers care about order

The order of an element is the number of additions needed to reach the identity again. Discrete-log protocols generally use large prime-order subgroups and validate public values. A large order is necessary but does not make every group secure.

order of g = smallest k > 0 with k·g ≡ 0 (divides n)

Where this touches your keys

##### The discrete-log family, in one skeleton

ECDH walks a generator point around an elliptic-curve group. [Diffie–Hellman](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/diffie-hellman.html>) walks a generator through residues mod a prime. Schnorr-style signatures live in prime-order groups by design. Once you see the **generator and its order**, every scheme in that family reads the same way.

Post-quantum standards use different assumptions: ML-KEM uses Module-LWE, ML-DSA uses module-lattice assumptions including Module-LWE and Module-SIS-related problems, and SLH-DSA uses hash-based constructions. Rings and modules also have underlying groups. Their expected quantum resistance is not proved merely by calling them something other than cyclic groups; it rests on the absence of efficient known attacks for the chosen problems and parameters.

Related: [Modular Arithmetic](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/modular-arithmetic.html>) is this exhibit's multiplicative cousin.

**Sources and scope:** NIST [FIPS 203](<https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/203/final>), [204](<https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/204/final>) and [205](<https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/205/final>) (2024).

#### Embedded script 1 (JavaScript)

Source text, not a record of every rendered state. Any ebook URL is replaced by a stable omission ID; redacted code is for inspection, not execution.

```javascript

(function(){
  "use strict";
  var NS="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg";
  function el(t,a){var e=document.createElementNS(NS,t);for(var k in a)e.setAttribute(k,a[k]);return e;}
  function gcd(a,b){while(b){var t=b;b=a%b;a=t;}return a;}

  var grp=document.getElementById("grp"), nsel=document.getElementById("nsel"),
      gsel=document.getElementById("gsel"), gread=document.getElementById("gread");
  var cx=350, cy=270, R=200;

  function pos(k,n){var ang=-Math.PI/2+2*Math.PI*k/n;return [cx+R*Math.cos(ang),cy+R*Math.sin(ang)];}
  function orbit(g,n){var out=[],x=0;for(var i=0;i<n;i++){x=(x+g)%n;out.push(x);if(x===0)break;}return out;}

  function refreshG(){
    var n=+nsel.value; gsel.innerHTML="";
    for(var g=1;g<n;g++){var o=document.createElement("option");o.value=g;
      o.textContent=g+(gcd(g,n)===1?" ✓":"");gsel.appendChild(o);}
    for(var g2=1;g2<n;g2++){if(gcd(g2,n)===1){gsel.value=g2;break;}}
  }
  function draw(animate){
    var n=+nsel.value, g=+gsel.value;
    grp.innerHTML="";
    grp.appendChild(el("circle",{cx:cx,cy:cy,r:R,fill:"none",stroke:"#2e2e3a","stroke-width":1.5}));
    for(var k=0;k<n;k++){var p=pos(k,n);
      grp.appendChild(el("circle",{cx:p[0],cy:p[1],r:4,fill:"#2e2e3a"}));
      var t=el("text",{x:cx+(R+24)*Math.cos(-Math.PI/2+2*Math.PI*k/n),
        y:cy+(R+24)*Math.sin(-Math.PI/2+2*Math.PI*k/n)+4,fill:"#D5CDDF",
        "font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"13","text-anchor":"middle"});
      t.textContent=k; grp.appendChild(t);
    }
    var orb=orbit(g,n);
    var full=(orb.length===n);
    var color=full?"#5AC8A0":"#C85A5A";
    var layer=el("g",{}); grp.appendChild(layer);
    function upto(m){
      layer.innerHTML="";
      var prev=pos(0,n);
      for(var i=0;i<m;i++){var p=pos(orb[i],n);
        layer.appendChild(el("line",{x1:prev[0],y1:prev[1],x2:p[0],y2:p[1],
          stroke:color,"stroke-width":2,"stroke-opacity":0.55})); prev=p;}
      for(var j=0;j<m;j++){var q=pos(orb[j],n);
        layer.appendChild(el("circle",{cx:q[0],cy:q[1],r:6,fill:color,stroke:"#1c1c22","stroke-width":1.5}));}
      if(m>0){var last=pos(orb[m-1],n);
        layer.appendChild(el("circle",{cx:last[0],cy:last[1],r:9,fill:color,stroke:"#1c1c22","stroke-width":2}));}
    }
    gread.innerHTML="⟨"+g+"⟩ in ℤ/"+n+" · order <b>"+orb.length+"</b> "+(full?"<b style='color:#5AC8A0'>(generator)</b>":"(subgroup)");
    if(animate&&!(window.matchMedia&&window.matchMedia("(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)").matches)){
      var m=0;var iv=setInterval(function(){upto(++m);if(m>=orb.length)clearInterval(iv);},110);
    }
    else upto(orb.length);
  }
  nsel.addEventListener("change",function(){refreshG();draw(false);});
  gsel.addEventListener("change",function(){draw(false);});
  document.getElementById("trace").addEventListener("click",function(){draw(true);});
  refreshG(); draw(false);
})();
```

[← Cipher Museum](<https://ciphermuseum.com/index.html>) Hall of Foundations

The Mathematics Under the Cryptography

### The ground your
keys *stand on*

Every modern cipher in the museum rests on mathematics far older than any protocol. This hall shows that bedrock — the number theory, the hardness assumptions, the structures — with a working interactive for each, and an honest account of where the math stops being something a cryptographer directly uses.

**What belongs here.** These are foundations, not primitives. Different schemes use different parts of this mathematics; some exhibits provide context rather than a security assumption. For the deployable tools, see [Modern Cryptography](<https://ciphermuseum.com/modern.html>) and [Hall XI](<https://ciphermuseum.com/halls/modern-crypto.html>). This hall is for understanding *why they work*.

#### The number theory ECC & RSA rest on

The arithmetic world where public-key cryptography actually happens.

[§142

##### Finite Fields

 𝔽ₚ · arithmetic mod a prime

Watch a smooth elliptic curve shatter into a scatter of dots — and explore finite-field curve arithmetic.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/finite-fields.html>) [§143

##### Modular Arithmetic

 ℤ/nℤ · clock arithmetic

Primitive roots and the cyclic group Diffie–Hellman lives inside.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/modular-arithmetic.html>) [§144

##### The Prime Number Theorem

 π(x) ~ x / ln x

Prime density explains the expected search for RSA key-generation candidates.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/prime-number-theorem.html>) [§145

##### Euler & Fermat

 aᵠ⁽ⁿ⁾ ≡ 1 (mod n), gcd(a,n)=1

The little theorem that makes RSA decryption undo RSA encryption. Watch a message round-trip.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/euler-fermat.html>)

#### Hardness assumptions — the honest bedrock

Cryptography rests on problems believed hard. Here is what that means.

[§146

##### The One-Way Function

 easy → · hard ←

A computational security idea: compare multiplication with a toy factoring search.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/one-way-function.html>) [§147

##### Lattices & the SVP

 ℤⁿ · shortest vector

Drag a basis and hunt the shortest vector — geometric intuition for the lattice-based post-quantum family.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/lattices-svp.html>) [§152

##### The Closest Vector Problem

 CVP · geometric decoding intuition

Round a target to the nearest lattice corner — exact with the orthogonal preset, potentially wrong with a skewed one. This is decoding.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/closest-vector.html>) [§148

##### Reduction & Proof

 A ≤ B · "as hard as"

What "provably as hard as factoring" actually claims — and what it quietly assumes.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/reduction.html>)

#### Structures the protocols assume

The vocabulary half the modern wing silently depends on.

[§149

##### Group Theory

 ⟨g⟩ · cyclic groups & order

Generators, order, and cyclic structure — the grammar of the discrete-log family.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/group-theory.html>) [§150

##### Elliptic Curves over ℝ

 y² = x³ + ax + b

A gentle first look at the group law, drawn by hand, before it moves to a finite field.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/elliptic-curves-real.html>) [§151

##### Information & Entropy

 H = −Σ p log p

Shannon's measure of surprise: why the one-time pad is perfect and why randomness assumptions matter.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/entropy.html>)

#### Post-quantum & advanced structures

Teaching models of structures used by standards and privacy protocols.

[§153

##### Polynomial Rings

 R_q = ℤ_q[X]/(Xⁿ+1)

The structured lattices inside ML-KEM and ML-DSA — watch polynomials wrap around the ring boundary.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/polynomial-rings.html>) [§154

##### The Random Oracle

 avalanche · Fiat–Shamir

Explore a toy avalanche effect, then distinguish it from the ideal random-function model used in Fiat–Shamir security arguments.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/random-oracle.html>) [§155

##### Polynomial Interpolation

 k distinct points fix degree at most k−1

Explore interpolation over the reals, then see why Shamir sharing needs finite fields and uniform random coefficients.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/interpolation.html>) [§156

##### Bilinear Pairings

 e(aP,bQ) = e(P,Q)ᵃᵇ

Explore bilinearity with a tiny exponent model, then see where actual pairings fit into proof systems.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/pairings.html>) [§157

##### Learning With Errors

 t = As + e

Compare exact elimination with noisy equations in a tiny model; ML-KEM adds larger structured parameters and further mechanisms.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/learning-with-errors.html>)

Companion exhibit: [The Ground Beneath the Curve](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/modularity-exhibit.html>) — the modularity theorem as mathematical context alongside ECC. Start there if you came from the Fermat's Last Theorem thread.

[← Hall of Foundations](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/index.html>) Exhibit 155 · k points fix a curve

The Math Behind Splitting a Secret

### Points that
*lock* a curve

At *k distinct x-coordinates*, the point values determine one polynomial of degree *at most k*−1. Fewer nonzero-coordinate points leave its intercept undetermined. That algebra supplies the reconstruction step in Shamir secret sharing.

**Interpolation geometry.** Nonzero, distinct share coordinates let you reconstruct the intercept once enough points are present. Perfect secrecy additionally needs the finite-field randomness assumptions described below.

§1

#### Drop points, watch the curve commit

The secret is the height where the curve crosses x = 0. Add points one at a time. With too few, infinitely many curves fit — the intercept is not uniquely determined. The moment you place the k-th point, a single polynomial locks in and the intercept is revealed.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="curve" viewBox="0 0 700 480" id="interp" role="img" aria-label="Points on a graph; when enough are placed a unique polynomial locks in and reveals the y-intercept secret."></svg>
```

threshold k
- 2

- 3 [selected]

- 4
  Add a share Clear

—

Each ink dot is a share. Below k shares the grey ghost curves show some of the many polynomials that still fit. At k shares the vermilion curve is the unique answer and the secret intercept appears. Drag any dot to move it.

Keyboard alternative: add a share at a chosen x by entering it here. add share at x =  [Input xIn: type=number; initial value=1]   Add share Pick a share, focus the move button, then arrow keys move it by 0.25 per press. The result is announced. move share   Move with arrow keys

§2

#### Recovering the secret with Lagrange

Lagrange interpolation reconstructs a polynomial from distinct-coordinate values. Real Shamir secret sharing uses a finite field, distinct nonzero coordinates, and independently uniform random coefficients. The real-number curves here illustrate interpolation only; they are not an information-theoretically secure sharing scheme.

secret = f(0) = Σ yᵢ · ∏ⱼ≠ᵢ (−xⱼ) / (xᵢ − xⱼ) (mod p)

Where this touches your keys

##### The threshold behind shared keys

Shamir's scheme uses this interpolation identity over a finite field. With proper randomness, any *k* of *n* shares recover the secret and fewer than *k* leave its prior distribution unchanged. Threshold signatures such as FROST add further protocol steps and security requirements.

Applied in: [Shamir's Secret Sharing](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/shamir-secret-sharing.html>) — a small finite-field demonstration of reconstruction and its limits.
Related foundations: [Finite Fields](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/finite-fields.html>) — the arithmetic the shares actually live in, without which the "curve" leaks information; [Information & Entropy](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/entropy.html>) — why *k*−1 shares reveal nothing at all, which is an information-theoretic claim rather than a computational one; [Polynomial Rings](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/polynomial-rings.html>) — the same objects put to a very different use. Tested logic (Lagrange interpolation over a field) in `crypto-algebra.js`.

**Sources and scope:** [Shamir, How to Share a Secret (1979)](<https://doi.org/10.1145/359168.359176>); [RFC 9591 (FROST, 2024)](<https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9591.html>).

#### Embedded script 1 (JavaScript)

Source text, not a record of every rendered state. Any ebook URL is replaced by a stable omission ID; redacted code is for inspection, not execution.

```javascript

(function(){
  "use strict";
  const NS="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg";
  const mk=(t,a)=>{const e=document.createElementNS(NS,t);for(const k in a)e.setAttribute(k,a[k]);return e;};
  const svg=document.getElementById("interp");
  const iread=document.getElementById("iread");
  const W=700,H=480,pad=54;
  // plot region: x in [0,7], y in [0,10]
  const xmin=0,xmax=7,ymin=0,ymax=10;
  const px=x=>pad+(x-xmin)/(xmax-xmin)*(W-2*pad);
  const py=y=>H-pad-(y-ymin)/(ymax-ymin)*(H-2*pad);

  let k=3;
  let pts=[[1,3],[3,7]]; // start below threshold
  let dragging=-1;

  // real-number Lagrange for smooth drawing (display), through given pts
  function lagrangePoly(points){
    return function(x){
      let sum=0;
      for(let i=0;i<points.length;i++){
        let term=points[i][1];
        for(let j=0;j<points.length;j++){ if(i===j)continue;
          term*=(x-points[j][0])/(points[i][0]-points[j][0]);
        }
        sum+=term;
      }
      return sum;
    };
  }

  function draw(){
    svg.innerHTML="";
    // axes
    svg.appendChild(mk("line",{x1:px(0),y1:py(ymin),x2:px(0),y2:py(ymax),stroke:"#F3EEDF","stroke-width":2}));
    svg.appendChild(mk("line",{x1:px(xmin),y1:py(0),x2:px(xmax),y2:py(0),stroke:"#2e2e3a","stroke-width":1}));
    // x=0 secret axis highlight
    svg.appendChild(mk("line",{x1:px(0),y1:py(ymin),x2:px(0),y2:py(ymax),stroke:"#C85A5A","stroke-width":1.5,"stroke-dasharray":"4 4","stroke-opacity":0.5}));
    const sl=mk("text",{x:px(0)-8,y:py(ymax)-6,fill:"#C85A5A","font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"12","text-anchor":"end"});
    sl.textContent="secret = f(0)"; svg.appendChild(sl);

    const enough=pts.length>=k;
    if(!enough && pts.length>=1){
      // draw several ghost curves that all pass through current points but differ
      for(let g=0;g<4;g++){
        let ghostX=6-g*0.7;
        while(pts.some(p=>Math.abs(p[0]-ghostX)<1e-8)) ghostX+=0.1;
        const extra=[...pts,[ghostX, 1+g*2.3]]; // keep all abscissas distinct
        if(extra.length>=2){
          const f=lagrangePoly(extra.slice(0,Math.max(pts.length+1,2)));
          let d="";
          for(let x=xmin;x<=xmax;x+=0.05){const y=f(x); if(y>=ymin-3&&y<=ymax+3){d+=(d?"L":"M")+px(x).toFixed(1)+" "+py(y).toFixed(1)+" ";}}
          svg.appendChild(mk("path",{d:d.trim(),fill:"none",stroke:"#C3A96F","stroke-width":1.2,"stroke-opacity":0.4}));
        }
      }
    }
    if(enough){
      const f=lagrangePoly(pts.slice(0,k));
      let d="";
      for(let x=xmin;x<=xmax;x+=0.03){const y=f(x); if(y>=ymin-3&&y<=ymax+3){d+=(d?"L":"M")+px(x).toFixed(1)+" "+py(y).toFixed(1)+" ";}}
      svg.appendChild(mk("path",{d:d.trim(),fill:"none",stroke:"#C85A5A","stroke-width":2.6}));
      // secret marker at x=0
      const secret=f(0);
      svg.appendChild(mk("circle",{cx:px(0),cy:py(secret),r:8,fill:"#C85A5A",stroke:"#1c1c22","stroke-width":2}));
      const st=mk("text",{x:px(0)+12,y:py(secret)+5,fill:"#C85A5A","font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"15","font-weight":"700"});
      st.textContent="\u2248 "+secret.toFixed(2); svg.appendChild(st);
    }
    // points
    pts.forEach((p,i)=>{
      svg.appendChild(mk("circle",{cx:px(p[0]),cy:py(p[1]),r:7,fill:"#F3EEDF",stroke:"#1c1c22","stroke-width":2,cursor:"grab"}));
    });

    if(enough){
      iread.innerHTML="<b>"+pts.length+"/"+k+"</b> shares \u00b7 curve locked \u00b7 <b style='color:var(--red)'>secret revealed</b>";
    } else {
      iread.innerHTML="<b>"+pts.length+"/"+k+"</b> shares \u00b7 <b>need "+(k-pts.length)+" more</b> \u00b7 secret still free";
    }
    refreshShareSel();
  }

  // keyboard drag: keep the share <select> in step with the current points
  const shareSel=document.getElementById("shareSel");
  function refreshShareSel(){
    const keep=shareSel.selectedIndex;
    while(shareSel.options.length>pts.length) shareSel.remove(shareSel.options.length-1);
    pts.forEach((p,i)=>{
      const text="share "+(i+1)+" ( "+p[0].toFixed(2)+", "+p[1].toFixed(2)+" )";
      if(i<shareSel.options.length) shareSel.options[i].textContent=text;
      else { const o=document.createElement("option"); o.value=i; o.textContent=text; shareSel.appendChild(o); }
    });
    if(keep>=0&&keep<pts.length) shareSel.selectedIndex=keep;
  }

  function nearestPt(evt){
    const rect=svg.getBoundingClientRect();
    const mx=((evt.touches?evt.touches[0].clientX:evt.clientX)-rect.left)*(W/rect.width);
    const my=((evt.touches?evt.touches[0].clientY:evt.clientY)-rect.top)*(H/rect.height);
    for(let i=0;i<pts.length;i++){ if(Math.hypot(mx-px(pts[i][0]),my-py(pts[i][1]))<24) return i; }
    return -1;
  }
  function moveTo(evt){
    if(dragging<0)return;
    const rect=svg.getBoundingClientRect();
    const mx=((evt.touches?evt.touches[0].clientX:evt.clientX)-rect.left)*(W/rect.width);
    const my=((evt.touches?evt.touches[0].clientY:evt.clientY)-rect.top)*(H/rect.height);
    let nx=xmin+(mx-pad)/(W-2*pad)*(xmax-xmin);
    let ny=ymin+(H-pad-my)/(H-2*pad)*(ymax-ymin);
    nx=Math.max(0.5,Math.min(xmax,nx)); ny=Math.max(ymin,Math.min(ymax,ny));
    // Keep x distinct so interpolation stays defined.
    if(pts.some((p,i)=>i!==dragging&&Math.abs(p[0]-nx)<1e-8)) return;
    pts[dragging]=[nx,ny]; draw();
  }
  svg.addEventListener("mousedown",e=>{ dragging=nearestPt(e); });
  svg.addEventListener("mousemove",moveTo);
  window.addEventListener("mouseup",()=>{dragging=-1;});
  svg.addEventListener("touchstart",e=>{ dragging=nearestPt(e); if(dragging>=0)e.preventDefault(); },{passive:false});
  svg.addEventListener("touchmove",e=>{ if(dragging>=0){moveTo(e);e.preventDefault();} },{passive:false});
  window.addEventListener("touchend",()=>{dragging=-1;});

  function addShare(atX){
    if(pts.length>=k){
      iread.textContent="The curve is already fixed by "+k+" shares. Clear it or raise the threshold to add another.";
      return;
    }
    // pick an x not already used
    let x=atX;
    if(x!=null&&(!Number.isFinite(x)||x<0.5||x>xmax)){
      iread.textContent="Choose a share coordinate between 0.5 and "+xmax+"; x=0 is the secret itself.";
      return;
    }
    if(x==null){ x=1; while(pts.some(p=>Math.abs(p[0]-x)<0.4)&&x<xmax) x++; }
    if(pts.some(p=>Math.abs(p[0]-x)<0.4)) return;
    pts.push([x, 2+Math.random()*6]);
    draw();
  }
  document.getElementById("ksel").addEventListener("change",e=>{ k=+e.target.value; pts=pts.slice(0,k); draw(); });
  document.getElementById("addPt").addEventListener("click",()=>addShare(null));
  document.getElementById("clr").addEventListener("click",()=>{ pts=[]; draw(); });
  document.getElementById("addAt").addEventListener("click",()=>{
    const x=parseInt(document.getElementById("xIn").value,10);
    if(!isNaN(x)) addShare(x);
  });

  // arrow keys on the move pad drag the selected share; the step size and new
  // position are announced through the aria-live readout
  const KB_STEP=0.25;
  document.getElementById("movePad").addEventListener("keydown",(e)=>{
    const i=shareSel.selectedIndex;
    if(i<0||i>=pts.length) return;
    let [nx,ny]=pts[i];
    if(e.key==="ArrowLeft") nx-=KB_STEP;
    else if(e.key==="ArrowRight") nx+=KB_STEP;
    else if(e.key==="ArrowUp") ny+=KB_STEP;
    else if(e.key==="ArrowDown") ny-=KB_STEP;
    else return;
    e.preventDefault();
    nx=Math.max(0.5,Math.min(xmax,nx)); ny=Math.max(ymin,Math.min(ymax,ny));
    if(pts.some((p,j)=>j!==i&&Math.abs(p[0]-nx)<1e-8)){
      iread.textContent="Shares need distinct x coordinates; that position is already occupied.";
      return;
    }
    pts[i]=[nx,ny]; draw();
    iread.innerHTML+=" · share "+(i+1)+" moved to ( <b>"+nx.toFixed(2)+"</b>, <b>"+ny.toFixed(2)+"</b> ), step "+KB_STEP;
  });
  draw();
})();
```

[← Hall of Foundations](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/index.html>) Exhibit 147 · shortest vector

The Foundation Under Post-Quantum Crypto

### A grid that
*hides* its shortcut

A lattice consists of integer combinations of basis vectors. The shortest nonzero vector can be hard to find in suitable high-dimensional instances; dimension alone does not make every lattice hard. Related structured lattice problems support ML-KEM and ML-DSA. Other post-quantum families, including hash-based signatures, have different foundations.

**Two views of a lattice.** A change of basis can obscure short vectors, but arbitrary dragging changes the lattice. The two presets hold the square lattice fixed. This two-dimensional model is efficiently solvable and does not establish cryptographic hardness.

§1

#### Two vectors, infinite grid

Pick two independent vectors b₁ and b₂. Their integer combinations form a lattice. Arbitrary dragging can change the lattice itself. Only invertible integer changes of basis with determinant ±1 preserve the same lattice. The good and bad preset buttons provide two bases for the same square lattice.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="curve" viewBox="0 0 700 520" id="lat" role="img" aria-label="A 2D lattice with draggable basis vectors and its shortest vector marked."></svg>
```

Reveal shortest vector Snap to good basis Snap to bad basis

—

Drag either arrowhead to change the basis. **Reveal** marks the true shortest vector (teal). A "bad" basis has long, nearly-parallel vectors that make it hard to spot.

Keyboard alternative: type integer components for each basis vector, then reveal the shortest vector. b₁  [Input b1x: type=number; initial value=2]   [Input b1y: type=number; initial value=1]   b₂  [Input b2x: type=number; initial value=1]   [Input b2y: type=number; initial value=2]   Set basis

§2

#### Easy in two dimensions, brutal in five hundred

In two dimensions efficient reduction algorithms find a shortest vector. In high dimensions, known algorithms for many relevant exact or approximation problems can be expensive. Cryptographic security still depends on the instance distribution, approximation target and parameters, not simply on a large dimension.

SVP: given a basis, find the shortest nonzero lattice vector

A two-dimensional picture motivates the problem; concrete security estimates require a specified distribution, dimension and approximation target.

Where this touches your keys

##### The lattice-based standards connect here

ML-KEM and ML-DSA use specific module-lattice assumptions, including Module-LWE and, for signatures, Module-SIS-related assumptions. Their keys contain short secret vectors and structured public equations rather than a generic hidden good basis. The picture offers geometric intuition; it is not a proof that breaking either standard is equivalent to solving arbitrary exact SVP.

**Three related teaching models.** [§147](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/lattices-svp.html>) introduces lattice geometry. [§152](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/closest-vector.html>) illustrates geometric decoding. [§157](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/learning-with-errors.html>) shows a tiny noisy-equation model and distinguishes it from standardized keys. Read them in that order.

### Scripts supporting this page
