#### Embedded script 1 (JavaScript)

Source text, not a record of every rendered state. Any ebook URL is replaced by a stable omission ID; redacted code is for inspection, not execution.

```javascript

(function(){
  "use strict";
  const LM = window.LatticeMath;
  const NS="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg";
  const el=(t,a)=>{const e=document.createElementNS(NS,t);for(const k in a)e.setAttribute(k,a[k]);return e;};
  const svg=document.getElementById("lat");
  const lread=document.getElementById("lread");
  const W=700,H=520, ox=350, oy=270, scale=42;

  // basis in lattice units (integer components keep the grid a true lattice)
  let b1={x:2,y:1}, b2={x:1,y:2};
  let dragging=null, showShort=false;

  const sx=u=>ox+u*scale, sy=v=>oy-v*scale;

  // sync the keyboard number inputs with the current basis
  function syncInputs(){
    document.getElementById("b1x").value=b1.x; document.getElementById("b1y").value=b1.y;
    document.getElementById("b2x").value=b2.x; document.getElementById("b2y").value=b2.y;
  }

  function draw(){
    svg.innerHTML="";
    const R=6;
    for(let i=-R;i<=R;i++)for(let j=-R;j<=R;j++){
      const x=i*b1.x+j*b2.x, y=i*b1.y+j*b2.y;
      const px=sx(x), py=sy(y);
      if(px<10||px>W-10||py<10||py>H-10) continue;
      const origin=(x===0&&y===0);
      svg.appendChild(el("circle",{cx:px,cy:py,r:origin?5:3,fill:origin?"#F3EEDF":"#C3A96F"}));
    }
    const drawVec=(b,label)=>{
      svg.appendChild(el("line",{x1:sx(0),y1:sy(0),x2:sx(b.x),y2:sy(b.y),stroke:"#C85A5A","stroke-width":2.5}));
      svg.appendChild(el("circle",{cx:sx(b.x),cy:sy(b.y),r:9,fill:"#C85A5A",stroke:"#1c1c22","stroke-width":2,cursor:"grab"}));
      const t=el("text",{x:sx(b.x)+13,y:sy(b.y)-11,fill:"#C85A5A","font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"15","font-weight":"700"});
      t.textContent=label; svg.appendChild(t);
    };
    drawVec(b1,"b\u2081"); drawVec(b2,"b\u2082");
    if(showShort){
      const s=LM.shortestVector([b1.x,b1.y],[b2.x,b2.y]);
      svg.appendChild(el("line",{x1:sx(0),y1:sy(0),x2:sx(s.x),y2:sy(s.y),stroke:"#5AC8A0","stroke-width":3.5,"stroke-opacity":0.9}));
      svg.appendChild(el("circle",{cx:sx(s.x),cy:sy(s.y),r:7,fill:"#5AC8A0",stroke:"#1c1c22","stroke-width":2}));
      lread.innerHTML="shortest = ("+s.x+", "+s.y+") \u00b7 length <b>"+s.len.toFixed(2)+"</b> = "+s.i+"\u00b7b\u2081 + "+s.j+"\u00b7b\u2082";
    } else {
      const det=Math.abs(LM.det2([b1.x,b1.y],[b2.x,b2.y]));
      lread.innerHTML="b\u2081=("+b1.x+","+b1.y+") b\u2082=("+b2.x+","+b2.y+") \u00b7 det <b>"+det+"</b>";
    }
  }

  function nearestUnit(evt){
    const rect=svg.getBoundingClientRect();
    const mx=((evt.touches?evt.touches[0].clientX:evt.clientX)-rect.left)*(W/rect.width);
    const my=((evt.touches?evt.touches[0].clientY:evt.clientY)-rect.top)*(H/rect.height);
    let ux=Math.round((mx-ox)/scale), uy=Math.round((oy-my)/scale);
    return {x:Math.max(-6,Math.min(6,ux)), y:Math.max(-6,Math.min(6,uy))};
  }
  function pick(evt){
    const rect=svg.getBoundingClientRect();
    const mx=((evt.touches?evt.touches[0].clientX:evt.clientX)-rect.left)*(W/rect.width);
    const my=((evt.touches?evt.touches[0].clientY:evt.clientY)-rect.top)*(H/rect.height);
    const d1=Math.hypot(mx-sx(b1.x),my-sy(b1.y)), d2=Math.hypot(mx-sx(b2.x),my-sy(b2.y));
    if(Math.min(d1,d2)>26) return null;
    return d1<d2?"b1":"b2";
  }
  function move(evt){
    if(!dragging)return;
    const u=nearestUnit(evt);
    if(u.x===0&&u.y===0) return;
    const tgt=dragging==="b1"?b1:b2, other=dragging==="b1"?b2:b1;
    if(LM.det2([u.x,u.y],[other.x,other.y])===0) return;
    tgt.x=u.x; tgt.y=u.y;
    showShort=false; syncInputs(); draw();
  }
  svg.addEventListener("mousedown",e=>{ dragging=pick(e); });
  svg.addEventListener("mousemove",move);
  window.addEventListener("mouseup",()=>{ dragging=null; });
  svg.addEventListener("touchstart",e=>{ dragging=pick(e); if(dragging)e.preventDefault(); },{passive:false});
  svg.addEventListener("touchmove",e=>{ if(dragging){move(e);e.preventDefault();} },{passive:false});
  window.addEventListener("touchend",()=>{ dragging=null; });

  document.getElementById("reveal").addEventListener("click",()=>{ showShort=true; draw(); });
  document.getElementById("good").addEventListener("click",()=>{ b1={x:1,y:0}; b2={x:0,y:1}; showShort=false; syncInputs(); draw(); });
  document.getElementById("bad").addEventListener("click",()=>{ b1={x:4,y:3}; b2={x:5,y:4}; showShort=false; syncInputs(); draw(); });

  // keyboard fallback: type basis components, guard against degenerate/collinear
  document.getElementById("applyBasis").addEventListener("click",()=>{
    const n1x=parseInt(document.getElementById("b1x").value,10), n1y=parseInt(document.getElementById("b1y").value,10);
    const n2x=parseInt(document.getElementById("b2x").value,10), n2y=parseInt(document.getElementById("b2y").value,10);
    if([n1x,n1y,n2x,n2y].some(v=>!Number.isFinite(v)||Math.abs(v)>6)){
      lread.textContent="Use integer components between −6 and 6 for this bounded demonstration.";
      return;
    }
    if(LM.det2([n1x,n1y],[n2x,n2y])===0){
      lread.innerHTML="<b style='color:var(--red)'>those vectors are parallel</b> \u2014 they collapse the lattice to a line. Try again.";
      return;
    }
    b1={x:n1x,y:n1y}; b2={x:n2x,y:n2y}; showShort=true; draw(); // reveal + announce
  });

  syncInputs(); draw();
})();
```

<a id="p176"></a>
## P176 — Learning With Errors · Hall of Foundations

- Site path: `hall-of-foundations/learning-with-errors.html`
- Public page: https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/learning-with-errors.html
- Snapshot source SHA-256: `ce67bdc0d5d3c4fcd25fc1ae7f4a4025577996efc7ab48020d049a9e2cb0cb74`
- Source identities in the prior evidence ledger: BONEH-SHOUP-2023, FIPS-203-204, MENEZES-LATTICES-2026

[← Hall of Foundations](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/index.html>) Exhibit 157 · t = As + e

A Teaching Model of Noisy Public Equations

### The secret hidden
by *a little noise*

A square linear system has a unique solution when its matrix is invertible. This demo samples invertible matrices modulo 97. With zero error, elimination recovers the secret. With nonzero error, solving the exact equations generally recovers a different vector; a tiny instance remains easy to attack by enumerating the possible secrets.

**A public key you can read.** The essential public relation behind ML-KEM is on this page: a public matrix, and a noisy product of that matrix with a short secret vector. A real key adds seeds, encodings and compression on top — §4 lists what this leaves out — but the relation is the part that carries the security. Set the error to zero and the secret falls out in one click, which is the fastest way to see what the error is for.

§1

#### A system anyone can solve

Below is a public matrix *A* and a public vector *t*, both with entries modulo *q* = 97. Hidden behind them is a short secret vector *s* — every entry is −1, 0, or 1. The relationship is the plainest one in mathematics:

t = A·s + e (mod 97)

Set the **error** to zero and that *e* vanishes, leaving *t* = *A*·*s*. Press **Solve** and Gaussian elimination — the same procedure you learned for three equations in three unknowns — recovers *s* instantly and exactly, every time. There is no security here at all.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="curve" viewBox="0 0 700 460" id="lwe" role="img" aria-label="The equation t equals A times s plus e, shown as a matrix, a secret column, an error column, and a result column."></svg>
```

error bound  [Input err: type=range; initial value=0]   Solve for s Reveal s New keys

—

The **error bound** is the largest value any entry of *e* may take, plus or minus. At **0** the system is exact and elimination wins. A bound of **1** permits small errors, including an all-zero draw. A nonzero error changes the exact solution; some coordinates may still match the secret.

Keyboard alternative: set the error bound with the slider, then activate Solve. Results are announced in the readout. error bound value  [Input errNum: type=number; initial value=0]   Apply

§2

#### Why so small a change ruins it

Elimination works by scaling rows and subtracting them from one another. Modulo a prime, "scaling" means multiplying by numbers like 43 or 88 — there is no notion of a *small* multiplier to keep things tidy. So a single unit of error, multiplied by 43 and added to another row's error, multiplied again at the next step, is smeared across the whole computation within a few rows.

For this invertible matrix, elimination always solves the displayed equations. With nonzero error modulo q, that exact solution differs from the original secret. A positive sampling bound can still produce an all-zero error vector.

Noise prevents exact elimination from being a general secret-recovery method. Security requires suitable dimensions, distributions and parameters: small error does not automatically make every instance hard. LWE and Module-LWE specify such sampling problems, with Module-LWE using structured modules over polynomial rings.

§3

#### The cancellation that lets the owner in

A hard problem alone is not cryptography — the legitimate recipient has to get through. Encryption picks fresh small randomness *r* and sends two things: a vector *u* = *A*ᵀ*r* + *e*₁, and a number *v* = *t*·*r* + *e*₂ plus the message bit multiplied by ⌊q/2⌋ — a value large enough to spot from across the ring.

Decryption computes *v* − *s*·*u*. Expand both sides and a large term appears twice, with opposite signs:

v − s·u = sᵀAᵀr + e·r + e₂ − sᵀAᵀr − s·e₁ + m·⌊q/2⌋ → **m·⌊q/2⌋ + small**

The bulk cancels. What survives is the message bit, sitting on top of a pile of error terms that were all small to begin with. Round to whichever of 0 or ⌊q/2⌋ is nearer, and the bit falls out. **Nothing here rounds with a private basis** — the secret's job is to make one big term disappear.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="curve" viewBox="0 0 700 400" id="dec" role="img" aria-label="Decryption arithmetic: the residual value after cancellation, shown against the noise budget."></svg>
```

send bit
- 0

- 1 [selected]
   error bound  [Input derr: type=range; initial value=1]   Encrypt & decrypt Run 200 times

—

The bar shows how far the decrypted value landed from the ideal **m·⌊q/2⌋** for the bit that was actually sent. The vermilion line is the budget, ⌊q/4⌋ = 24; the track runs to 48, the furthest a value can sit from the sent ideal. An error strictly below 24 guarantees correct decoding here; at 24 the outcome depends on the bit, direction and tie rule. Larger errors decode incorrectly. Raise the error and watch the bar climb.

Keyboard alternative: choose a bit and an error bound, then activate Encrypt and decrypt. The residual noise and recovered bit are announced. error bound value  [Input derrNum: type=number; initial value=1]   Apply

§4

#### Honest limits of this model

Three things here are toys, and it is worth naming them.

- The size4 × 4, q = 97ML-KEM works over a 256-coefficient polynomial ring with q = 3329, arranged in modules of rank 2 to 4. The ternary secret here has only 81 possibilities; it is here to be readable, not hard.

- The randomnessuniform, not GaussianML-KEM draws relevant small values from centered binomial distributions; other schemes use different distributions, and the security proofs depend on that shape. This exhibit picks uniformly in a range, which makes the slider legible but is not what gets deployed.

- The schemeone bit, no KEMThis is an LWE-inspired one-bit encryption model, not a standard-compatible implementation. ML-KEM is a key-encapsulation mechanism with compression, a Fujisaki–Okamoto transform for chosen-ciphertext security, and rejection steps this page has no equivalent of.

What is faithful is the shape: the public key really is a matrix plus a noisy product, the secret really is a short vector, decryption really does work by cancelling a shared term, and the noise budget really is the thing parameter choices are fighting over.

§5

#### From an encrypted bit to a shared key

A key-encapsulation mechanism establishes a shared secret for later symmetric cryptography. Its three operations have different inputs and outputs:

- **Key generation:** the recipient creates a public encapsulation key and a private decapsulation key.

- **Encapsulation:** using an authentic copy of that public key, the sender produces a secret shared key *K* and a public ciphertext *c*. Only the ciphertext is sent.

- **Decapsulation:** the recipient uses the private key and ciphertext to recover the shared key. Receiving key-shaped bytes alone does not authenticate the sender or confirm that both parties agree.

In ML-KEM's Fujisaki–Okamoto construction, decapsulation decrypts a candidate message, derives a candidate key and encryption randomness, then **reencrypts** that message. It compares the regenerated ciphertext with the received one. A match selects the candidate key; a mismatch selects the fallback *J(z ∥ c)*, derived from a private value *z* stored in the decapsulation key and the received ciphertext.

This is **implicit rejection**: the internal consistency failure still yields key bytes. The fallback is deterministic for the same private key and ciphertext, not fresh randomness drawn on each failure. The real interface does not return the internal rejection flag. A malformed ciphertext length fails a separate input check before this branch. Correct protocol integration must handle such errors and provide the authentication or key confirmation its application requires.

Step through the existing [KEM Trap lab](<https://systemslibrarian.github.io/crypto-lab-kem-trap/>) for reencryption, implicit rejection and caller mistakes. Its separate shortened-comparison example is a schematic implementation bug, not a break of correct ML-KEM. This explanation adds no standard-compatible algorithm to the one-bit demo above.

For the geometric connection behind the equations, see the [worked rectangular LWE lattice example](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/closest-vector.html#lwe-lattice>). It uses a different matrix shape from this page's square, small-secret toy.

Where this touches your keys

##### The bet the post-quantum standards make

ML-KEM uses this broad structured noisy-equation pattern at much larger parameters, with additional operations omitted here. No efficient classical or quantum attack is known for its intended security targets. That expectation is based on cryptanalysis of the specified problems, not a proof that merely adding noise eliminates every exploitable quantum structure.

**Three related teaching models.** [§147](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/lattices-svp.html>) introduces lattice geometry. [§152](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/closest-vector.html>) illustrates geometric decoding. [§157](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/learning-with-errors.html>) shows a tiny noisy-equation model and distinguishes it from standardized keys. Next: [Polynomial Rings](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/polynomial-rings.html>), where these plain vectors become polynomials. Tested logic in `lwe-math.js`.

**Sources and scope:** Alfred Menezes, *A Gentle Introduction to Lattice-Based Cryptography*, version 3.3, September 16, 2026, §6.4, pp.64–66, Algorithm 6.12; NIST [FIPS 203 (ML-KEM, 2024)](<https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/203/final>), Algorithms 16–21, and [FIPS 204 (ML-DSA, 2024)](<https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/204/final>). The KEM explanation is conceptual. This scalar, uniform-error toy is not either standard.

#### Embedded script 1 (JavaScript)

Source text, not a record of every rendered state. Any ebook URL is replaced by a stable omission ID; redacted code is for inspection, not execution.

```javascript

(function(){
  "use strict";
  var L = window.LWEMath;
  var NS = "http://www.w3.org/2000/svg";
  function mk(t,a){ var e=document.createElementNS(NS,t); for(var k in a) e.setAttribute(k,a[k]); return e; }
  function txt(x,y,s,fill,anchor,weight){
    var t=mk("text",{x:x,y:y,fill:fill||"var(--ink)","font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace",
      "font-size":"15","text-anchor":anchor||"middle","font-weight":weight||"400"});
    t.textContent=s; return t;
  }

  var Q=97, N=4;
  var svg=document.getElementById("lwe"), lread=document.getElementById("lread");
  var errS=document.getElementById("err"), errNum=document.getElementById("errNum");
  var A=[], s=[], e=[], t=[], shown=false, solved=null;

  function ri(lo,hi){ return lo+Math.floor(Math.random()*(hi-lo+1)); }

  function regen(){
    // resample A until it is invertible mod q, so "Solve" always has an answer
    var guard=0;
    do {
      A=[]; for(var i=0;i<N;i++){ var r=[]; for(var j=0;j<N;j++) r.push(ri(0,Q-1)); A.push(r); }
      guard++;
    } while(L.gaussianSolve(A,[0,0,0,0],Q)===null && guard<50);
    s=[]; for(var k=0;k<N;k++) s.push(ri(-1,1));
    reErr(); solved=null; shown=false;
  }
  function reErr(){
    var b=+errS.value;
    e=[]; for(var i=0;i<N;i++) e.push(b===0?0:ri(-b,b));
    t=L.lweSample(A,s,e,Q);
    solved=null;
  }

  function draw(){
    while(svg.firstChild) svg.removeChild(svg.firstChild);
    var cw=44, ch=44, ax=48, ay=150;
    // headers
    svg.appendChild(txt(ax+cw*2, ay-26, "A  (public)", "var(--muted)"));
    svg.appendChild(txt(ax+cw*4+62, ay-26, "s", "var(--vermilion)", "middle", "700"));
    svg.appendChild(txt(ax+cw*4+166, ay-26, "e", "var(--teal)", "middle", "700"));
    svg.appendChild(txt(ax+cw*4+272, ay-26, "t  (public)", "var(--muted)"));

    // A grid
    for(var i=0;i<N;i++) for(var j=0;j<N;j++){
      svg.appendChild(mk("rect",{x:ax+j*cw,y:ay+i*ch,width:cw-3,height:ch-3,
        fill:"none",stroke:"var(--line,#3a3a44)","stroke-width":"1",rx:"3"}));
      svg.appendChild(txt(ax+j*cw+(cw-3)/2, ay+i*ch+27, String(A[i][j]), "var(--ink-soft)"));
    }
    svg.appendChild(txt(ax+cw*4+18, ay+2*ch+27, "×", "var(--muted)"));

    function col(x,vals,color,secret){
      for(var i=0;i<N;i++){
        svg.appendChild(mk("rect",{x:x,y:ay+i*ch,width:cw-3,height:ch-3,fill:"none",
          stroke:color,"stroke-width":"1.5",rx:"3"}));
        var label = (secret && !shown) ? "?" : String(vals[i]);
        svg.appendChild(txt(x+(cw-3)/2, ay+i*ch+27, label, color, "middle", "700"));
      }
    }
    col(ax+cw*4+40, s, "var(--vermilion)", true);
    svg.appendChild(txt(ax+cw*4+122, ay+2*ch+27, "+", "var(--muted)"));
    col(ax+cw*4+144, e, "var(--teal)", false);
    svg.appendChild(txt(ax+cw*4+226, ay+2*ch+27, "=", "var(--muted)"));
    col(ax+cw*4+250, t, "var(--ink-soft)", false);

    // solved row
    if(solved){
      var sy=ay+N*ch+44;
      svg.appendChild(txt(ax, sy, "elimination returns:", "var(--muted)", "start"));
      var match=true;
      for(var m=0;m<N;m++) if(solved[m]!==L.mod(s[m],Q)) match=false;
      for(var c=0;c<N;c++){
        var cc = match ? "var(--teal)" : "var(--vermilion)";
        svg.appendChild(mk("rect",{x:ax+190+c*cw,y:sy-24,width:cw-3,height:32,fill:"none",
          stroke:cc,"stroke-width":"1.5",rx:"3"}));
        svg.appendChild(txt(ax+190+c*cw+(cw-3)/2, sy-1, String(L.center(solved[c],Q)), cc, "middle", "700"));
      }
      svg.appendChild(txt(ax+190+cw*4+16, sy-1,
        match ? "✓ that is s" : "✗ not s", match?"var(--teal)":"var(--vermilion)", "start", "700"));
    }
    var b=+errS.value;
    svg.appendChild(txt(350, 420, b===0 ? "error bound 0 — an exact linear system"
      : "error bound ±"+b+" — equations sampled with bounded noise", "var(--muted)"));
  }

  function report(){
    var b=+errS.value;
    if(!solved){ lread.textContent = b===0
      ? "Exact system. Press Solve." : "Error ±"+b+" applied. Press Solve."; return; }
    var match=true;
    for(var m=0;m<N;m++) if(solved[m]!==L.mod(s[m],Q)) match=false;
    lread.innerHTML = match
      ? "<b>Recovered s exactly.</b> With no error this is just algebra."
      : "<b>Wrong.</b> Elimination returned a confident answer that is not s.";
  }

  errS.addEventListener("input",function(){ errNum.value=errS.value; reErr(); draw(); report(); });
  document.getElementById("applyErr").addEventListener("click",function(){
    var v=Math.max(0,Math.min(24,+errNum.value||0));
    errS.value=v; errNum.value=v; reErr(); draw(); report();
  });
  document.getElementById("solve").addEventListener("click",function(){
    solved=L.gaussianSolve(A,t,Q); draw(); report();
  });
  document.getElementById("reveal").addEventListener("click",function(){
    shown=!shown; this.textContent = shown ? "Hide s" : "Reveal s"; draw();
  });
  document.getElementById("fresh").addEventListener("click",function(){
    regen(); draw(); report();
  });
  regen(); draw(); report();

  // ---------- §3 decryption ----------
  var dsvg=document.getElementById("dec"), dread=document.getElementById("dread");
  var derr=document.getElementById("derr"), derrNum=document.getElementById("derrNum");
  var bitSel=document.getElementById("bit");
  var last=null;

  function trial(bound,m){
    var AA=[], i, j;
    var guard=0;
    do{
      AA=[]; for(i=0;i<N;i++){ var r=[]; for(j=0;j<N;j++) r.push(ri(0,Q-1)); AA.push(r); }
      guard++;
    } while(L.gaussianSolve(AA,[0,0,0,0],Q)===null && guard<50);
    var ss=[], ee=[], rr=[], e1=[];
    for(i=0;i<N;i++){ ss.push(ri(-1,1)); ee.push(ri(-bound,bound)); rr.push(ri(-1,1)); e1.push(ri(-bound,bound)); }
    var tt=L.lweSample(AA,ss,ee,Q);
    var ct=L.encryptBit(AA,tt,m,rr,e1,ri(-bound,bound),Q);
    var out=L.decryptBit(ct.u,ct.v,ss,Q);
    // measure the error against the bit we SENT — decryptBit's own `noise` is
    // measured against the bit it decoded, which understates a failure.
    return { out:out, m:m, resid:Math.abs(L.residualNoise(out.raw,m,Q)) };
  }

  function drawDec(){
    while(dsvg.firstChild) dsvg.removeChild(dsvg.firstChild);
    var budget=L.noiseBudget(Q); // 24
    var x0=90, x1=610, y=250, w=x1-x0;
    dsvg.appendChild(txt(350, 54, "residual after cancelling sᵀAᵀr", "var(--muted)"));

    if(!last){
      dsvg.appendChild(txt(350, y, "Press “Encrypt & decrypt”", "var(--muted)"));
      return;
    }
    // Scale the track to the largest residual possible, q/2 — so an
    // over-budget result visibly crosses the line instead of pinning at it.
    var full=Math.floor(Q/2);
    var n=last.resid, frac=Math.min(1, n/full);
    var bx=x0+w*(budget/full);
    dsvg.appendChild(mk("rect",{x:x0,y:y-22,width:w,height:44,fill:"none",
      stroke:"var(--line,#3a3a44)","stroke-width":"1",rx:"4"}));
    var ok = last.out.bit===last.m;
    dsvg.appendChild(mk("rect",{x:x0+2,y:y-20,width:Math.max(2,(w-4)*frac),height:40,
      fill: ok?"var(--teal)":"var(--vermilion)","fill-opacity":"0.35"}));
    // budget line, mid-track
    dsvg.appendChild(mk("line",{x1:bx,y1:y-34,x2:bx,y2:y+34,
      stroke:"var(--vermilion)","stroke-width":"2"}));
    dsvg.appendChild(txt(bx, y+54, "budget ⌊q/4⌋ = "+budget, "var(--vermilion)"));
    dsvg.appendChild(txt(x0, y+54, "0", "var(--muted)"));
    dsvg.appendChild(txt(x1, y+54, String(full), "var(--muted)"));
    dsvg.appendChild(txt(350, y+6, "error from ideal = "+n, ok?"var(--teal)":"var(--vermilion)","middle","700"));

    dsvg.appendChild(txt(350, 130, "sent m = "+last.m+"   →   decrypted "+last.out.bit,
      ok?"var(--teal)":"var(--vermilion)","middle","700"));
    dsvg.appendChild(txt(350, 160, "v − s·u = "+last.out.raw+
      "   (centered "+last.out.centered+")", "var(--ink-soft)"));
    dsvg.appendChild(txt(350, 340, ok ? (n < budget ? "inside the guaranteed margin — rounding lands on the right bit" : "boundary case — rounding is correct for these inputs")
      : "rounding boundary crossed — recovered the wrong bit",
      ok?"var(--muted)":"var(--vermilion)"));
  }

  function send(){
    last=trial(+derr.value, +bitSel.value);
    drawDec();
    dread.innerHTML = last.out.bit===last.m
      ? "Decrypted <b>"+last.out.bit+"</b> — correct. Accumulated error "+last.resid+", budget 24."
      : "Decrypted <b>"+last.out.bit+"</b> — <b>wrong</b>. Accumulated error "+last.resid+
        " is outside the guaranteed margin (strictly below 24); rounding chose the other bit.";
  }
  derr.addEventListener("input",function(){ derrNum.value=derr.value; });
  document.getElementById("applyDerr").addEventListener("click",function(){
    var v=Math.max(1,Math.min(24,+derrNum.value||1)); derr.value=v; derrNum.value=v; send();
  });
  document.getElementById("send").addEventListener("click",send);
  document.getElementById("run100").addEventListener("click",function(){
    var ok=0, T=200, b=+derr.value;
    for(var i=0;i<T;i++){ var r=trial(b,i%2); if(r.out.bit===r.m) ok++; }
    dread.innerHTML="At error ±"+b+": <b>"+ok+"/"+T+"</b> decryptions correct ("+
      Math.round(100*ok/T)+"%).";
  });
  drawDec();
})();
```

[← Hall of Foundations](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/index.html>) Exhibit 143 · ℤ/nℤ

The Cyclic World Diffie–Hellman Lives In

### Arithmetic on
a *clock*

A clock counts to twelve and starts over. Modular arithmetic does the same with any modulus. Powers modulo a prime reveal cyclic groups and subgroups; deployed key exchanges often use a large prime-order subgroup rather than every nonzero residue.

**Why this matters.** A primitive root generates every nonzero residue modulo a prime, but finite-field Diffie–Hellman can instead use a generator of a large prime-order subgroup. Group order, parameter selection and validation matter; being a primitive root alone does not establish security.

§1

#### Powers that walk the clock

Fix a prime *p* and a base *g*. Compute g¹, g², g³, … mod p and mark each result on the ring below. If *g* is a primitive root, the marks visit all p−1 positions before returning home — a single unbroken orbit. If not, the orbit closes early, missing most of the clock.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="curve" viewBox="0 0 700 520" id="clock" role="img" aria-label="Powers of g modulo p plotted around a clock face."></svg>
```

prime p
- 11

- 13

- 23 [selected]

- 29
   base g   Trace the orbit

—

A green ring means **g is a primitive root** — its powers cover every residue. A short red loop means it doesn't. Try g = 5 vs g = 2 at p = 23.

§2

#### The one-way staircase

Computing gˣ mod p is efficient. Recovering an exponent from the result is the discrete logarithm problem. This tiny group is easy to search; suitable large groups make known attacks expensive. The result constrains the exponent modulo the order of g.

x ↦ gˣ mod p easy · gˣ ↦ x hard

Where this touches your keys

##### The original public-key setting

In finite-field Diffie–Hellman, Alice sends gᵃ and Bob sends gᵇ; each computes gᵃᵇ. Computing that value as an eavesdropper is the computational Diffie–Hellman problem. Solving a discrete logarithm would suffice, but discrete-log hardness alone is not a proof of CDH hardness.

Many deployed finite-field DH groups use a safe prime and a large prime-order subgroup. This avoids treating small-order elements as acceptable public keys, provided implementations also apply the protocol's public-key validation rules. Choosing a generator alone does not prevent an active attacker from sending an invalid or small-order value.

See also: [Group Theory](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/group-theory.html>) for the general structure, and [Finite Fields](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/finite-fields.html>) for the elliptic-curve version.

**Sources and scope:** [RFC 7919 (2016)](<https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7919.html>), standardized finite-field DH groups and validation; Boneh and Shoup, §10.4.2, CDH and discrete logarithms.

#### Embedded script 1 (JavaScript)

Source text, not a record of every rendered state. Any ebook URL is replaced by a stable omission ID; redacted code is for inspection, not execution.

```javascript

(function(){
  "use strict";
  var NS="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg";
  function el(t,a){var e=document.createElementNS(NS,t);for(var k in a)e.setAttribute(k,a[k]);return e;}
  // Primitive-root logic lives in crypto-algebra.js, proven in crypto-algebra.test.js.
  var isPrimRoot=window.CryptoAlgebra.isPrimitiveRoot, powers=window.CryptoAlgebra.powersOf;

  var clock=document.getElementById("clock");
  var psel=document.getElementById("psel"), gsel=document.getElementById("gsel");
  var read=document.getElementById("read");
  var cx=350, cy=270, R=200;

  function pos(residue,p){
    var ang=-Math.PI/2 + (2*Math.PI*residue)/p; // place residue r at angle
    return [cx+R*Math.cos(ang), cy+R*Math.sin(ang)];
  }

  function refreshG(){
    var p=+psel.value; gsel.innerHTML="";
    for(var g=2;g<p;g++){ var o=document.createElement("option"); o.value=g;
      o.textContent=g+(isPrimRoot(g,p)?" ✓":""); gsel.appendChild(o); }
    // default to a primitive root
    for(var g2=2;g2<p;g2++){ if(isPrimRoot(g2,p)){ gsel.value=g2; break; } }
  }

  function draw(animate){
    var p=+psel.value, g=+gsel.value;
    clock.innerHTML="";
    clock.appendChild(el("circle",{cx:cx,cy:cy,r:R,fill:"none",stroke:"#2e2e3a","stroke-width":1.5}));
    // residue ticks 0..p-1
    for(var r=0;r<p;r++){
      var pt=pos(r,p);
      clock.appendChild(el("circle",{cx:pt[0],cy:pt[1],r:3,fill:"#2e2e3a"}));
      if(p<=29){
        var t=el("text",{x:cx+(R+22)*Math.cos(-Math.PI/2+2*Math.PI*r/p),
          y:cy+(R+22)*Math.sin(-Math.PI/2+2*Math.PI*r/p)+4,
          fill:"#D5CDDF","font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"12",
          "text-anchor":"middle"});
        t.textContent=r; clock.appendChild(t);
      }
    }
    var prim=isPrimRoot(g,p);
    var color=prim?"#5AC8A0":"#C85A5A";
    var seq=powers(g,p);
    // draw the orbit as connected chords
    var pathG=el("g",{});
    clock.appendChild(pathG);
    function drawUpTo(n){
      pathG.innerHTML="";
      var prev=pos(1,p); // g^0 = 1 start
      for(var i=0;i<n;i++){
        var pt=pos(seq[i],p);
        pathG.appendChild(el("line",{x1:prev[0],y1:prev[1],x2:pt[0],y2:pt[1],
          stroke:color,"stroke-width":1.6,"stroke-opacity":0.55}));
        prev=pt;
      }
      // mark visited
      for(var j=0;j<n;j++){ var q=pos(seq[j],p);
        pathG.appendChild(el("circle",{cx:q[0],cy:q[1],r:5,fill:color,stroke:"#1c1c22","stroke-width":1.5})); }
      // current highlighted
      if(n>0){ var last=pos(seq[n-1],p);
        pathG.appendChild(el("circle",{cx:last[0],cy:last[1],r:8,fill:color,stroke:"#1c1c22","stroke-width":2}));
        var lt=el("text",{x:last[0]+12,y:last[1]-10,fill:color,
          "font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"14","font-weight":"700"});
        lt.textContent="g^"+n; pathG.appendChild(lt);
      }
    }
    read.innerHTML="g = <b>"+g+"</b> mod "+p+" · "+(prim?"<b style='color:#5AC8A0'>primitive root</b>":"order "+new Set(seq).size);
    if(animate&&!(window.matchMedia&&window.matchMedia("(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)").matches)){
      var n=0; var iv=setInterval(function(){ drawUpTo(++n); if(n>=seq.length)clearInterval(iv); },70);
    }
    else drawUpTo(seq.length);
  }

  psel.addEventListener("change",function(){ refreshG(); draw(false); });
  gsel.addEventListener("change",function(){ draw(false); });
  document.getElementById("anim").addEventListener("click",function(){ draw(true); });

  refreshG(); draw(false);
})();
```

[← Hall of Foundations](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/index.html>) Hall of Foundations · Exhibit 141

The Number Theory Beneath ECC

### The ground beneath
the *curve*

Elliptic-curve cryptography rests on objects far older and stranger than any key exchange. This exhibit walks from a cubic equation to the deepest theorem it touches — and is honest about where the mathematics stops being useful to a cryptographer.

**A note on honesty.** Fermat's Last Theorem — the 100-page proof this exhibit builds toward — has no direct use in cryptography. The link between elliptic curves and modular forms is mathematical context here. ECC’s group law and computational assumptions do not depend on the Modularity Theorem.

§1

#### A curve is a cubic

For this exhibit an elliptic curve is a nonsingular Weierstrass cubic together with a point at infinity. Its equation has rational coefficients, while this smooth plot shows its real solutions. Rational, real and finite-field points are different sets. The name comes from the history of elliptic integrals.

y² = x³ + ax + b

Fix *a* and *b* so the curve is smooth — no cusps, no self-crossings — and you get the graceful looping shape below. What makes it cryptographically interesting is not the shape but a hidden operation living on it: you can *add* two points and land on a third.

§2

#### The group law, drawn by hand

Here is the whole secret. To add points *P* and *Q*: draw the line through them, find the third place it meets the curve, and reflect that point across the x-axis. That reflection is *P + Q*. Tap two points and watch the construction draw itself.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="curve" viewBox="0 0 700 460" id="stage" role="img" aria-label="Interactive elliptic curve. Tap points to add them.">
          <!-- axes + curve drawn by JS -->
          <g id="grid"></g>
          <path id="curvePath" fill="none" stroke="#F3EEDF" stroke-width="2.4"></path>
          <g id="construction"></g>
          <g id="points"></g>
        </svg>
```

Pick two points Reset

y² = x³ + x + 1 · **tap the curve**

tap once for *P*, again for *Q* — the chord, the third intersection, and the reflection to *P+Q* all draw in vermilion.

Keyboard alternative: choose two points on the curve to add. Point P   Point Q   Add P + Q

Continue into the working mathematics: [Elliptic Curves over ℝ](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/elliptic-curves-real.html>) takes this same group law and draws it by hand, and [Finite Fields](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/finite-fields.html>) shows the smooth curve shattering into the scatter cryptography actually uses. Teaching model using floating-point approximations: point coordinates are computed from the actual group law over the reals and projected onto the display; no cryptographic protocol is implemented.

§3

#### Scalar multiplication and its inverse problem

In an elliptic-curve group, scalar multiplication forms *Q = kP* by adding *k* copies of *P*. Recovering *k* modulo the order of *P* from *P* and *Q* is the elliptic-curve discrete logarithm problem. Its difficulty depends on the group and parameters.

P, Q known → find k such that Q = kP · difficulty depends on the group

Suitable finite-field elliptic-curve groups support hard discrete-log and related assumptions used by protocols such as ECDH and ECDSA. Not every curve or prime is secure, and visual scattering does not prove hardness. The modularity story below is mathematical context rather than a prerequisite for that security claim.

§4

#### The second face: modular forms

An elliptic curve over ℚ has an L-function built from arithmetic data, including point counts at primes of good reduction. Modular forms are highly symmetric functions on the complex upper half-plane. Their connection is deep number theory, not an extra encryption operation.

The Modularity Theorem associates every elliptic curve over ℚ with a weight-two cuspidal newform whose L-function matches that of the curve. Wiles and Taylor–Wiles established the semistable case in work published in 1995; the general case was completed by 2001. This is an equality of L-functions, not a claim that a zeta function literally is a modular form.

Two traditions that never spoke — point-counting and the analysis of symmetric functions — turned out to be the same language.

§5

#### The rungs to Fermat

Modularity is where Fermat's Last Theorem falls. Not by attacking *aⁿ + bⁿ = cⁿ* directly, but by showing a hypothetical counterexample would build an elliptic curve too strange to exist.

- Frey · 1985the trap A counterexample to Fermat yields a curve y² = x(x + aⁿ)(x − bⁿ) with impossible properties.

- Serre · Ribetthe tightening Made precise: such a curve, if modular, forces a modular form that plainly cannot exist.

- Wiles · 1994the keystone Proved semistable curves are modular — so the Frey curve would be modular — so it cannot exist — so Fermat holds.

The proof runs 109 pages plus a 20-page companion. Its engine is an equality between a deformation ring and a Hecke ring — "R = T" — reached through commutative algebra, not through the curves themselves.

Where this touches your keys

##### Mathematical context, not a security assumption

ECC does not require the Modularity Theorem or Fermat's Last Theorem for its group law, correctness or security assumption. The theorem is valuable mathematical context. Practical ECC depends on finite-field arithmetic, suitable group parameters and protocol-specific computational assumptions; modularity does not prove that those assumptions are hard.

This exhibit is reference and education. For the deployable primitives — ECDH, ECDSA, and post-quantum successors — see [Modern Cryptography](<https://ciphermuseum.com/modern.html>).

#### Primary mathematical sources

Andrew Wiles, [*Modular elliptic curves and Fermat’s Last Theorem*](<https://annals.math.princeton.edu/1995/141-3/p01>), Annals of Mathematics 141 (1995), 443–551; Richard Taylor and Andrew Wiles, [*Ring-theoretic properties of certain Hecke algebras*](<https://annals.math.princeton.edu/1995/141-3/p02>), 553–572. These are the 109-page paper and 20-page companion referenced above. Christophe Breuil, Brian Conrad, Fred Diamond and Richard Taylor, *On the Modularity of Elliptic Curves over ℚ: Wild 3-adic Exercises*, Journal of the American Mathematical Society 14 (2001), 843–939, establishes the general result. The illustration does not reproduce these proofs.

#### Embedded script 1 (JavaScript)

Source text, not a record of every rendered state. Any ebook URL is replaced by a stable omission ID; redacted code is for inspection, not execution.

```javascript

(function () {
  "use strict";
  const svg = document.getElementById("stage");
  const NS = "http://www.w3.org/2000/svg";
  const W = 700, H = 460;

  // curve: y^2 = x^3 + Ax + B  (A=1, B=1 -> smooth, single connected branch)
  const A = 1, B = 1;

  // world<->screen mapping
  const xmin = -1.4, xmax = 3.2, ymin = -4.2, ymax = 4.2;
  const sx = (x) => (x - xmin) / (xmax - xmin) * W;
  const sy = (y) => H - (y - ymin) / (ymax - ymin) * H;

  function make(tag, attrs) {
    const e = document.createElementNS(NS, tag);
    for (const k in attrs) e.setAttribute(k, attrs[k]);
    return e;
  }

  // ---- grid + axes ----
  const grid = document.getElementById("grid");
  function axisLine(x1, y1, x2, y2, w, op) {
    grid.appendChild(make("line", { x1, y1, x2, y2,
      stroke: "#2e2e3a", "stroke-width": w, "stroke-opacity": op }));
  }
  for (let gx = Math.ceil(xmin); gx <= xmax; gx++)
    axisLine(sx(gx), 0, sx(gx), H, 1, gx === 0 ? 0.9 : 0.35);
  for (let gy = Math.ceil(ymin); gy <= ymax; gy++)
    axisLine(0, sy(gy), W, sy(gy), 1, gy === 0 ? 0.9 : 0.35);

  // ---- curve path (single connected branch for A=1, B=1) ----
  const fx = (x) => x * x * x + A * x + B;
  const topBranch = [], botBranch = [], step = 0.012;
  for (let x = xmin; x <= xmax; x += step) {
    const v = fx(x);
    if (v >= 0) {
      const r = Math.sqrt(v);
      topBranch.push([x, r]);
      botBranch.push([x, -r]);
    }
  }
  let d = "";
  topBranch.forEach((p, i) => { d += (i ? "L" : "M") + sx(p[0]).toFixed(1) + " " + sy(p[1]).toFixed(1) + " "; });
  for (let j = botBranch.length - 1; j >= 0; j--) {
    d += "L" + sx(botBranch[j][0]).toFixed(1) + " " + sy(botBranch[j][1]).toFixed(1) + " ";
  }
  document.getElementById("curvePath").setAttribute("d", d.trim());

  // ---- interaction state ----
  const cons = document.getElementById("construction");
  const pts = document.getElementById("points");
  const readout = document.getElementById("readout");
  let picks = []; // world-space points on curve

  function onCurveNear(px, py) {
    let best = null, bestd = Infinity;
    for (let xx = xmin; xx <= xmax; xx += 0.004) {
      const v = fx(xx);
      if (v < 0) continue;
      const r = Math.sqrt(v);
      for (const c of [[xx, r], [xx, -r]]) {
        const dd = (sx(c[0]) - px) ** 2 + (sy(c[1]) - py) ** 2;
        if (dd < bestd) { bestd = dd; best = c; }
      }
    }
    return best;
  }

  const dot = (x, y, color, r) => make("circle",
    { cx: sx(x), cy: sy(y), r: r || 6, fill: color, stroke: "#1c1c22", "stroke-width": 2 });

  function label(x, y, txt, color) {
    const t = make("text", { x: sx(x) + 11, y: sy(y) - 9, fill: color,
      "font-family": "ui-monospace, monospace", "font-size": "15", "font-weight": "700" });
    t.textContent = txt;
    return t;
  }

  // add two points via chord-and-tangent (handles the doubling case)
  function addPoints(P, Q) {
    if (Math.abs(P[0]-Q[0]) < 1e-9 && Math.abs(P[1]+Q[1]) < 1e-9) {
      return { third: null, sum: null, slope: null };
    }
    let m;
    if (Math.abs(P[0] - Q[0]) < 1e-9 && Math.abs(P[1] - Q[1]) < 1e-9) {
      m = (3 * P[0] * P[0] + A) / (2 * P[1]);      // tangent slope (P = Q)
    } else {
      m = (Q[1] - P[1]) / (Q[0] - P[0]);           // chord slope
    }
    const x3 = m * m - P[0] - Q[0];
    const y3 = m * (P[0] - x3) - P[1];
    return { third: [x3, -y3], sum: [x3, y3], slope: m };
  }

  function drawConstruction() {
    cons.innerHTML = "";
    if (picks.length < 2) return;
    const [P, Q] = picks;
    const { third, sum, slope: m } = addPoints(P, Q);

    if (sum === null) {
      cons.appendChild(make("line", {x1:sx(P[0]), y1:0, x2:sx(P[0]), y2:H, stroke:"#C85A5A", "stroke-width":2}));
      readout.textContent = "P + Q = ∞ — the point at infinity (the group identity).";
      return;
    }
    const yL = P[1] + m * (xmin - P[0]);
    const yR = P[1] + m * (xmax - P[0]);
    cons.appendChild(make("line", { x1: sx(xmin), y1: sy(yL), x2: sx(xmax), y2: sy(yR),
      stroke: "#C85A5A", "stroke-width": 2, "stroke-opacity": 0.85 }));

    cons.appendChild(dot(third[0], third[1], "#d98a7a", 5));

    cons.appendChild(make("line", { x1: sx(third[0]), y1: sy(third[1]),
      x2: sx(sum[0]), y2: sy(sum[1]),
      stroke: "#C85A5A", "stroke-width": 1.6, "stroke-dasharray": "4 4", "stroke-opacity": 0.8 }));

    cons.appendChild(dot(sum[0], sum[1], "#C85A5A", 7));
    cons.appendChild(label(sum[0], sum[1], "P+Q", "#C85A5A"));

    // aria-live readout announces the full result to screen readers
    readout.innerHTML = "P+Q = ( <b>" + sum[0].toFixed(2) + "</b>, <b>" + sum[1].toFixed(2) + "</b> )";
  }

  function redrawPoints() {
    pts.innerHTML = "";
    picks.forEach((p, i) => {
      pts.appendChild(dot(p[0], p[1], "#F3EEDF", 6));
      pts.appendChild(label(p[0], p[1], i === 0 ? "P" : "Q", "#F3EEDF"));
    });
  }

  // register a chosen point, update visuals + announce
  function choose(p) {
    if (!p) return;
    if (picks.length >= 2) { picks = []; cons.innerHTML = ""; }
    picks.push(p);
    redrawPoints();
    if (picks.length === 2) {
      drawConstruction();
    } else {
      readout.innerHTML = "P = ( <b>" + p[0].toFixed(2) + "</b>, <b>" +
        p[1].toFixed(2) + "</b> ) &nbsp;·&nbsp; select a second point for Q";
    }
  }

  function pointFromEvent(clientX, clientY) {
    const rect = svg.getBoundingClientRect();
    const cx = (clientX - rect.left) * (W / rect.width);
    const cy = (clientY - rect.top) * (H / rect.height);
    return onCurveNear(cx, cy);
  }

  // ---- pointer (mouse) ----
  svg.addEventListener("click", (e) => choose(pointFromEvent(e.clientX, e.clientY)));

  // ---- touch: only intercept a deliberate tap, never a scroll ----
  // Track the start point; if the finger moves beyond a small threshold we treat
  // it as a scroll and leave the page's default behaviour untouched.
  let touchStart = null;
  const TAP_SLOP = 12; // px of movement still counted as a tap

  svg.addEventListener("touchstart", (e) => {
    if (e.touches.length !== 1) { touchStart = null; return; }
    const t = e.touches[0];
    touchStart = { x: t.clientX, y: t.clientY, scrolled: false };
    // note: do NOT preventDefault here — that would trap a scroll gesture
  }, { passive: true });

  svg.addEventListener("touchmove", (e) => {
    if (!touchStart) return;
    const t = e.touches[0];
    if (Math.hypot(t.clientX - touchStart.x, t.clientY - touchStart.y) > TAP_SLOP) {
      touchStart.scrolled = true; // it's a scroll — let the page move
    }
  }, { passive: true });

  svg.addEventListener("touchend", (e) => {
    if (!touchStart || touchStart.scrolled) { touchStart = null; return; }
    // confirmed tap: now it's safe to suppress the emulated click and act
    e.preventDefault();
    const t = e.changedTouches[0];
    choose(pointFromEvent(t.clientX, t.clientY));
    touchStart = null;
  }, { passive: false });

  // ---- reset ----
  document.getElementById("reset").addEventListener("click", () => {
    picks = [];
    cons.innerHTML = "";
    pts.innerHTML = "";
    readout.innerHTML = "y² = x³ + x + 1 &nbsp;·&nbsp; <b>tap the curve</b>";
  });

  // ---- preload a demonstrative pair ----
  document.getElementById("pick").addEventListener("click", () => {
    const Px = -0.6, Qx = 1.2;
    picks = [[Px, Math.sqrt(fx(Px))], [Qx, -Math.sqrt(fx(Qx))]];
    redrawPoints();
    drawConstruction();
  });

  // ---- keyboard / screen-reader fallback ----
  // Populate two <select>s with real on-curve points; adding announces via aria-live.
  const selP = document.getElementById("selP");
  const selQ = document.getElementById("selQ");
  const sampleXs = [-0.6, -0.2, 0.4, 1.0, 1.6, 2.4]; // spread across the domain
  const kbPoints = [];
  sampleXs.forEach((x) => {
    const v = fx(x);
    if (v < 0) return;
    const r = Math.sqrt(v);
    kbPoints.push([x, r]);
    kbPoints.push([x, -r]);
  });
  kbPoints.forEach((p, i) => {
    const text = "( " + p[0].toFixed(2) + ", " + p[1].toFixed(2) + " )";
    const oP = document.createElement("option"); oP.value = i; oP.textContent = text; selP.appendChild(oP);
    const oQ = document.createElement("option"); oQ.value = i; oQ.textContent = text; selQ.appendChild(oQ);
  });
  selP.value = "0";
  selQ.value = String(Math.min(3, kbPoints.length - 1));

  document.getElementById("addKb").addEventListener("click", () => {
    const P = kbPoints[+selP.value];
    const Q = kbPoints[+selQ.value];
    picks = [P, Q];
    redrawPoints();
    drawConstruction(); // updates the aria-live readout with P+Q
  });
})();
```

[← Hall of Foundations](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/index.html>) Exhibit 146 · easy → · hard ←

The Conceptual Keystone

### Easy one way,
*hard* the other

Many computational cryptographic constructions depend on functions that are easy to evaluate and difficult to invert on properly sampled inputs. Their security is conjectured, not proved merely by drawing an arrow. Information-theoretic systems such as the one-time pad do not require this computational assumption.

**An assumption, not a proof.** We *believe* these functions are hard to reverse; we cannot prove it. Computational public-key schemes rely on particular hardness assumptions. Some proposed schemes have been broken, so each construction needs its own analysis.

§1

#### Multiply, then try to undo it

Multiplying suitably chosen large primes and attempting to recover them illustrates a candidate one-way relationship. For the tiny values shown, factoring is easy. The plot compares multiplication with a trial-division estimate, not with the fastest known general factoring algorithms.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="curve" viewBox="0 0 700 380" id="owf" role="img" aria-label="Cost of multiplying versus factoring as numbers grow."></svg>
```

size (bits)  [Input br: type=range; initial value=16]   Factor it (watch it crawl)

—

The bar estimates trial division up to √n. The Factor button counts actual trial divisions for this tiny sample. More advanced factoring algorithms are substantially faster at large sizes; do not extrapolate this bar into an RSA security estimate.

§2

#### The trapdoor twist

Some public-key constructions add a trapdoor. RSA's relevant operation is modular exponentiation: knowing the prime factors lets the owner compute a private exponent and invert that operation. Decryption is not division of the ciphertext by a prime.

public: n = p·q · trapdoor: the pair (p, q)

Trapdoor functions are one design pattern, not a description of every public-key scheme. Diffie–Hellman key agreement, for example, has a different structure.

Where this touches your keys

##### The bet under everything

Factoring breaks RSA, and solving discrete logs breaks classical DH and ECC. Post-quantum designs use several families, including lattice, code and hash-based assumptions. Security claims must name the particular construction and problem, rather than treating all these as the same trapdoor.

A cryptographic hash is intended to resist preimage search without a secret inversion trapdoor. This does not mean an attacker cannot recover a low-entropy input by guessing. No proof establishes that a concrete hash such as SHA-256 is a one-way function against every efficient attacker.

Next: [Reduction & Proof](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/reduction.html>) — how we argue one wall is at least as tall as another.

Continue with [RSA](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/rsa.html>) or explore [security reductions](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/reduction.html>).

#### Embedded script 1 (JavaScript)

Source text, not a record of every rendered state. Any ebook URL is replaced by a stable omission ID; redacted code is for inspection, not execution.

```javascript

(function(){
  "use strict";
  var NS="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg";
  function el(t,a){var e=document.createElementNS(NS,t);for(var k in a)e.setAttribute(k,a[k]);return e;}
  function randPrime(bits){
    var lo=Math.pow(2,bits-1), hi=Math.pow(2,bits)-1;
    function isP(n){if(n<2)return false;for(var i=2;i*i<=n;i++)if(n%i===0)return false;return true;}
    for(var tries=0;tries<9999;tries++){var c=Math.floor(lo+Math.random()*(hi-lo));c|=1;if(isP(c))return c;}
    return 3;
  }
  var owf=document.getElementById("owf"), br=document.getElementById("br"), oread=document.getElementById("oread");
  var W=700,H=380,pad=54;

  function draw(){
    var bits=+br.value; owf.innerHTML="";
    var half=Math.max(2,Math.floor(bits/2));
    var p=randPrime(half), q=randPrime(half), n=p*q;
    // forward cost ~ constant; factoring cost ~ sqrt(n) trial divisions (worst case bar)
    var fwd=1;
    var facCost=Math.sqrt(n);
    var maxBar=Math.sqrt(Math.pow(2,48));
    function barH(v){ return Math.min(1, Math.log(v+1)/Math.log(maxBar+1))*(H-2*pad); }
    owf.appendChild(el("line",{x1:pad,y1:H-pad,x2:W-pad,y2:H-pad,stroke:"#F3EEDF","stroke-width":1.5}));
    // forward bar
    var fh=Math.max(6,barH(fwd));
    owf.appendChild(el("rect",{x:180,y:H-pad-fh,width:120,height:fh,fill:"#5AC8A0"}));
    var t1=el("text",{x:240,y:H-pad+22,fill:"#5AC8A0","font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"13","text-anchor":"middle","font-weight":"700"});
    t1.textContent="multiply"; owf.appendChild(t1);
    // factor bar
    var gh=Math.max(6,barH(facCost));
    owf.appendChild(el("rect",{x:400,y:H-pad-gh,width:120,height:gh,fill:"#C85A5A"}));
    var t2=el("text",{x:460,y:H-pad+22,fill:"#C85A5A","font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"13","text-anchor":"middle","font-weight":"700"});
    t2.textContent="factor"; owf.appendChild(t2);
    // n label
    var tn=el("text",{x:W/2,y:pad-14,fill:"#F3EEDF","font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"15","text-anchor":"middle","font-weight":"700"});
    tn.textContent=p+" × "+q+" = "+n; owf.appendChild(tn);
    oread.innerHTML="n = <b>"+n+"</b> ("+(Math.floor(Math.log2(n))+1)+" bits) · trial-division estimate ≈ √n ≈ <b>"+Math.round(facCost)+"</b> steps";
    owf._current={n:n,p:p,q:q};
  }
  function runFactor(){
    var st=owf._current; if(!st)return; var n=st.n, count=0, found=null;
    for(var i=2;i*i<=n;i++){ count++; if(n%i===0){ found=[i,n/i]; break; } }
    oread.innerHTML="factored n = <b>"+n+"</b> = "+found[0]+" × "+found[1]+" after <b>"+count+"</b> trial divisions";
  }
  br.addEventListener("input",draw);
  document.getElementById("fac").addEventListener("click",runFactor);
  draw();
})();
```

## P180 — Bilinear Pairings · Hall of Foundations

[← Hall of Foundations](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/index.html>) Exhibit 156 · e(aP,bQ)=e(P,Q)ᵃᵇ

A Tool Used by Some zk-SNARKs

### Multiplying the
*unmultipliable*

Pairings connect elliptic-curve groups to a target group so that scaling two input points becomes exponentiation by the product of their scalars. This algebraic relationship supports some proof and signature schemes. Hiding those scalars requires suitable source and target groups and security assumptions.

**A model of one identity.** This page illustrates bilinearity with small modular powers. It neither hides its inputs nor implements a pairing or privacy protocol.

§1

#### A bridge between two worlds

A bilinear pairing e takes two points from elliptic-curve groups and maps them into a finite field, in a way that turns point-scaling into exponentiation. Scale the inputs by hidden scalars a and b, and the output is the base pairing raised to the *product* a·b.

e(aP, bQ) = e(P, Q)<sup>a·b</sup>

The identity enables checks on products of scalars without directly computing those scalars. It does not by itself establish zero knowledge or prevent attacks in either group.

§2

#### Explore the bilinearity identity

This interaction is an **exponent model of bilinearity**. It compares (5ᵃ)ᵇ mod 23 with 5ᵃᵇ mod 23 using the displayed scalars. It does not compute elliptic-curve points, a real pairing, or a zero-knowledge proof. The symbolic labels show where those objects occur in the actual pairing identity.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="curve" viewBox="0 0 700 470" id="pair" role="img" aria-label="Two computation paths that both arrive at e(P,Q) to the power ab, showing the pairing identity."></svg>
```

a  [Input aR: type=range; initial value=3]   b  [Input bR: type=range; initial value=5]

—

Both paths compute the same tiny modular exponentiation. This checks an exponent identity; the displayed P, Q and e(P,Q) are symbolic stand-ins, not calculated elliptic-curve points or pairing values.

Keyboard alternative: set scalars a and b, then evaluate both paths. a =  [Input aIn: type=number; initial value=3]   b =  [Input bIn: type=number; initial value=5]   Evaluate both paths

§3

#### How proof systems use pairings

Pairings enable compact verification in systems such as Groth16 and appear in KZG polynomial commitments. BLS signature aggregation is another application, but is not itself a zk-SNARK. Zero knowledge requires additional protocol structure and randomization; bilinearity or a polynomial commitment alone does not provide it.

A pairing checks an algebraic relationship. The surrounding protocol must supply soundness, zero knowledge and any required setup guarantees.

Pairings power several of the most influential and compact proof systems, but they are one major family rather than a requirement for succinctness. STARKs verify with hashes and no pairing at all; Bulletproofs and Halo-style systems get short proofs and recursion from ordinary discrete-log groups. The distinction matters here more than most places in this hall: a sufficiently capable fault-tolerant quantum computer running Shor’s algorithm would defeat the discrete-log assumptions used by pairing-friendly curves. Hash-based proof systems are candidates for post-quantum use, subject to their specific construction, parameters and quantum security analysis. Succinct verification has more than one foundation, and they don't share a fate.

Where this touches your keys

##### Under the privacy protocols

Some zk-SNARKs, identity-based encryption constructions and signature schemes use pairings. The target is typically a multiplicative subgroup of an extension field. The required security assumptions and parameter choices concern both source and target groups; a pairing is an algebraic tool, not an independent promise of privacy.

Applied in: [Zero-Knowledge Proofs](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/zero-knowledge-proofs.html>) — where the pairing-based zk-SNARK family puts this identity to work.
Builds on: [Elliptic Curves](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/elliptic-curves-real.html>), [Group Theory](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/group-theory.html>). Exponent identity tested in `crypto-algebra.js`.

**Sources and scope:** [Groth, On the Size of Pairing-based Non-interactive Arguments (2016)](<https://eprint.iacr.org/2016/260>); [Boneh, Lynn and Shacham, Short Signatures from the Weil Pairing (2001)](<https://crypto.stanford.edu/~dabo/pubs/papers/weilsigs.pdf>).

#### Embedded script 1 (JavaScript)

Source text, not a record of every rendered state. Any ebook URL is replaced by a stable omission ID; redacted code is for inspection, not execution.

```javascript

(function(){
  "use strict";
  const CA=window.CryptoAlgebra;
  const NS="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg";
  const mk=(t,a)=>{const e=document.createElementNS(NS,t);for(const k in a)e.setAttribute(k,a[k]);return e;};
  const pair=document.getElementById("pair");
  const pread=document.getElementById("pread");
  const aR=document.getElementById("aR"), bR=document.getElementById("bR");
  const aIn=document.getElementById("aIn"), bIn=document.getElementById("bIn");

  const BASE=5, MOD=23; // toy target group: e(P,Q) modelled as BASE in Z_23*

  function node(x,y,label,val,color){
    const g=mk("g",{});
    g.appendChild(mk("rect",{x:x-64,y:y-26,width:128,height:52,rx:4,fill:"#1c1c22",stroke:color,"stroke-width":2}));
    const l=mk("text",{x,y:y-32,fill:"#D5CDDF","font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"11","text-anchor":"middle"});
    l.textContent=label; g.appendChild(l);
    const v=mk("text",{x,y:y+7,fill:color,"font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"18","font-weight":"700","text-anchor":"middle"});
    v.textContent=val; g.appendChild(v);
    pair.appendChild(g); return g;
  }
  function arrow(x1,y1,x2,y2,label){
    pair.appendChild(mk("line",{x1,y1,x2,y2,stroke:"#F3EEDF","stroke-width":1.8}));
    const ang=Math.atan2(y2-y1,x2-x1);
    pair.appendChild(mk("polygon",{points:(x2)+","+(y2)+" "+(x2-10*Math.cos(ang-0.4))+","+(y2-10*Math.sin(ang-0.4))+" "+(x2-10*Math.cos(ang+0.4))+","+(y2-10*Math.sin(ang+0.4)),fill:"#F3EEDF"}));
    if(label){const t=mk("text",{x:(x1+x2)/2,y:(y1+y2)/2-8,fill:"#D4B765","font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"12","text-anchor":"middle","font-weight":"700"});t.textContent=label;pair.appendChild(t);}
  }

  function draw(a,b){
    pair.innerHTML="";
    const r=CA.pairingCheck(a,b,BASE,MOD);
    // LEFT PATH: scale points then pair
    node(150,90,"P, Q (base points)","P, Q","#F3EEDF");
    arrow(150,116,150,164,"scale by a, b");
    node(150,190,"aP, bQ (scalars hidden)","aP, bQ","#C85A5A");
    arrow(150,216,300,300,"pair e(\u00b7,\u00b7)");
    // RIGHT PATH: pair then exponentiate
    node(550,90,"e(P, Q) base pairing","e(P,Q) = "+BASE,"#5AC8A0");
    arrow(550,116,550,164,"raise to a\u00b7b = "+(a*b));
    node(550,190,"e(P,Q)^(ab)","= "+r.viaProduct,"#5AC8A0");
    arrow(550,216,400,300,"");
    // MEETING POINT
    const meet=node(350,340,"both paths agree",""+r.viaProduct,r.equal?"#5AC8A0":"#C85A5A");
    // check mark
    const chk=mk("text",{x:350,y:400,fill:r.equal?"#5AC8A0":"#C85A5A","font-family":"ui-monospace,monospace","font-size":"15","font-weight":"700","text-anchor":"middle"});
    chk.textContent=r.equal?"\u2713 e(aP,bQ) = e(P,Q)^(ab) = "+r.viaProduct:"mismatch"; pair.appendChild(chk);

    pread.innerHTML="a="+a+", b="+b+" \u00b7 e(aP,bQ) = <b>"+r.viaChain+"</b> = e(P,Q)^"+(a*b)+" = <b>"+r.viaProduct+"</b> "+(r.equal?"<b style='color:#5AC8A0'>\u2713</b>":"\u2717");
    aIn.value=a; bIn.value=b; aR.value=a; bR.value=b;
  }
  function fromSliders(){ draw(+aR.value,+bR.value); }
  aR.addEventListener("input",fromSliders);
  bR.addEventListener("input",fromSliders);
  document.getElementById("evalBtn").addEventListener("click",()=>{
    let a=parseInt(aIn.value,10), b=parseInt(bIn.value,10);
    a=Math.max(1,Math.min(10,isNaN(a)?3:a)); b=Math.max(1,Math.min(10,isNaN(b)?5:b));
    draw(a,b);
  });
  draw(3,5);
})();
```
